#9615. Cinga Samson "Ukuphuthelwa" @ White Cube Gallery, NYC

South African artist Cinga Samson’s exhibition of new paintings is titled ‘Ukuphuthelwa’, an isiXhosa word in the artist’s native language that translates as ‘unable to sleep’. Unlike the English word ‘insomnia’, the isiXhosa term carries no negative connotation and accordingly, for Samson, sleeplessness is not a condition to be cured but a state of spiritual alertness, a sensitivity that deepens in the dark. Rendered in the artist’s signature occluded palette of near-blacks, carbon and deep Prussian blues, Samson’s scenes depict men-like figures, dogs in overgrown fields and portraits of plant life native to South Africa. Encouraging slow, contemplative looking, a sense of existential gravity pervades Samson’s new series of oil paintings, attempting to depict a vast reality that is continuously…

#9616. Nuart Aberdeen 2026: Poetry In The Streets

Welcome to the latest edition of Nuart Aberdeen. As far as we can ascertain, this will be the first street art festival in the world with a focus primarily on poetry and text-based works. Over the years, for better or worse, the large scale colourful figurative mural has come to dominate the culture we work with, and although it’s an aspect of the culture we support, due to the resources required to produce murals, they’re perhaps also the least democratic form of art on the streets. As curators, researchers and producers working in “festival” culture, we have a responsibility to not only showcase and celebrate the most interesting and technically competent works of our time, but to also ensure the…

#9629. Mysterious brain activity under anesthesia challenges what we know about consciousness

Even under general anesthesia, the human brain can analyze language, recognize different types of words, and predict what comes next in a story, according to a surprising discovery by Baylor researchers. The findings challenge our understanding of consciousness and suggest that the unconscious brain is far more capable than scientists previously realized.

#9630. Yale scientists discover how stress changes the brain to make memories stronger

Yale scientists have uncovered how the stress hormone cortisol helps the brain turn emotional experiences into stronger, longer-lasting memories. The discovery reveals that stress can reshape communication between brain networks involved in emotion and memory, potentially explaining why certain moments are so difficult to forget.

#9631. The secret to handling stress better may start at breakfast

Your daily routine could have a bigger impact on your ability to handle stress than you realize. A Binghamton University study of approximately 400 college students found that eating breakfast regularly, exercising, sleeping well, and taking fish oil were associated with greater psychological flexibility, which helps people adapt to stressful situations. Poor sleep and unhealthy eating habits, meanwhile, were linked to lower flexibility and resilience.

#9632. Scientists uncover a hidden map of the brain with 539 distinct regions

Scientists have uncovered a hidden layer of organization in the brain by creating the first detailed 3D map of its lipids, the fatty molecules essential for brain structure and communication. By analyzing millions of measurements from mouse brains, researchers identified 539 distinct chemical zones, revealing patterns and boundaries that traditional brain maps have missed. Surprisingly, even white matter, once thought to be relatively uniform, contains a complex patchwork of chemically distinct regions.

#9633. Scientists find hidden driver of aging and a simple amino acid that fights memory loss in mice

Scientists discovered that declining levels of a protein called Menin may help drive aging, and restoring it in older mice reversed several age-related changes in just 30 days. Even more surprisingly, supplementing with a simple amino acid called D-serine improved memory and learning, offering a potential new approach to age-related cognitive decline.

#9634. Just 10 minutes of daily exercise could unlock deeper sleep

Getting better sleep might be as simple as moving a little more every day. Researchers at The University of Texas at Austin found that people who exercised more frequently experienced more deep, restorative sleep, which is essential for brain recovery, memory, and emotional well-being. Surprisingly, just 10 minutes of moderate to vigorous exercise per day was associated with measurable benefits, while even light activities like walking and standing breaks were linked to better sleep and mood.

#9635. Scientists discover 5 hidden brain patterns behind depression

Depression may not be one biological condition, but several distinct patterns of brain activity hiding under the same diagnosis. Researchers studying 263 people with depression identified five brain profiles, each showing different patterns of communication between brain regions and associations with symptoms such as anxiety, trauma, and substance abuse.

#9636. After 30 years, scientists finally have a drug for a deadly brain disease

The FDA has approved the first drug that directly targets Alexander disease, a devastating neurological disorder affecting fewer than one in a million people. Developed through decades of research, Zanvastro showed promising results in clinical trials, stabilizing motor function and even improving movement in some patients.

#9637. Loneliness may steal nearly 6 years of good health

A large study suggests loneliness and social isolation can cost people years of healthy life, especially when it comes to mental health. Men experiencing both lost about 5.8 years free from depression and anxiety, while women lost roughly 3.7 years.

#9638. Drug target could block brain inflammation linked to Alzheimer’s and Parkinson’s

Blocking a receptor called P2X7 significantly reduced inflammation in human brain tissue, revealing a potential new treatment strategy for neurological and psychiatric disorders. The discovery could help researchers repurpose existing drugs for conditions including traumatic brain injury, Alzheimer’s, Parkinson’s, depression, and schizophrenia.

#9639. What happens in the brain when cannabis makes you anxious?

Cannabinoids may intensify anxiety by removing a natural brake on specific neurons in the brain’s fear center, particularly during stressful situations. In mice, shutting down those neurons sharply reduced cannabinoid-related anxious behavior.

#9640. Vagus Nerve Stimulation Could Help New Skills Stick

Stimulating the vagus nerve after practice helped mice develop stronger long-term motor learning, revealing a potentially important window when the brain is still locking in a new skill. The effect was linked to rhythmic changes in brain blood vessels, hinting that body-to-brain signals may help prepare the brain for lasting change.

#9641. This diet may slow brain aging by 2.5 years

People who followed the MIND diet more closely showed slower brain shrinkage and less grey matter loss over time, with the strongest differences equivalent to about 2.5 years of delayed brain aging. Berries and poultry were linked with healthier brain changes, while sweets and fried fast foods were associated with greater tissue loss.

#9642. Could quitting ultra-processed food boost your mental health?

People with depression who dramatically cut ultra-processed foods experienced notable improvements in their symptoms in a small preliminary study. Researchers suspect healthier eating may benefit the brain partly by reducing gut damage and chronic inflammation.

#9643. Brain scans reveal which disorders are linked to faster brain aging

A massive MRI study found that Alzheimer’s disease, mild cognitive impairment, psychiatric disorders, and addiction are associated with distinct patterns of accelerated brain aging. The strongest effects appeared in Alzheimer’s and mild cognitive impairment, while ADHD and autism showed no significant overall difference.

#9644. Children at risk for depression get “stuck” on sad faces

Depression may change which emotional faces children notice most, but the pattern appears to depend on family history. Children at higher familial risk became increasingly focused on sad faces, while lower-risk children with rising symptoms paid less attention to happy ones.

#9645. Scientists just discovered what coffee really does to your gut and brain

A new study suggests coffee may influence mood and brain function by changing the activity of microbes in the gut. Both caffeinated and decaf coffee were linked to lower stress, depression, and impulsivity, while also producing measurable changes in gut bacteria and metabolites. Decaf was associated with better learning and memory, while caffeinated coffee appeared to reduce anxiety and improve attention.

#9646. When what you see doesn’t make sense, your brain does something remarkable

The brain may have a built-in way to quickly settle disagreements between regions processing the same scene. Researchers found that matching signals between two visual areas lasted longer, while conflicting signals rapidly faded away. This “consensus building” mechanism could help explain how the brain turns many specialized inputs into one unified view of the world.

#9647. Coffee doesn’t have to keep you awake to disrupt your sleep

Caffeine may make sleep less restorative even when you fall asleep normally and stay in bed for eight hours, with EEG recordings revealing fewer of the slow brain waves associated with deep sleep. Individual sensitivity varies dramatically, meaning even caffeine consumed earlier in the day could affect nighttime recovery for some people.

#9648. Scientists create a needle-thin brain implant that can do three jobs at once

A new needle-thin brain implant can record neural activity, deliver drugs, and stimulate multiple brain regions using a single flexible fiber. Successful tests in mice suggest it could give scientists a more precise, less invasive way to study the brain and may eventually help treat neurological disorders such as epilepsy.

#9649. Scientists restore a brain protein and reverse signs of aging in mice

Scientists found that declining levels of the brain protein Menin may help drive aging, with lower levels in mice linked to inflammation, cognitive decline, weaker bones, and thinner skin. Restoring Menin reversed several age-related changes, while the amino acid D-serine improved cognition, hinting at a potentially intriguing new target for healthy aging.

#9650. Hidden brain wiring may help keep the mind sharp as gray matter shrinks

Scientists have discovered that the brain’s short-range wiring may help protect cognition even as gray matter shrinks with age. Healthier connections just beneath the brain’s surface were linked to better language skills and appeared to weaken the impact of gray matter loss.

#9651. Scientists find a new layer of Alzheimer’s hidden in the genome

Scientists found that the 3D organization of DNA is disrupted in several types of brain cells affected by Alzheimer’s disease, altering how important genes are switched on and off. The discovery reveals a previously underexplored layer of the disease that could open new paths for understanding and eventually treating Alzheimer’s.

#9652. A longer exhale may push your brain toward bolder decisions

Slow breathing with a prolonged exhale made people more willing to take risks by changing heart activity and increasing the brain’s sensitivity to rewards. The findings suggest that something as simple as controlling your breath can directly influence how you evaluate choices.

#9653. Scientists reveal the hidden instructions that build the human brain

UCLA researchers have discovered two powerful influences that help guide how the human brain forms before birth. Radial glia, stem cells responsible for producing much of the cerebral cortex, change their behavior depending on how they process glucose and on physical contact with signals arriving from the thalamus. Those signals can alter which types of neurons are produced, including upper-layer neurons that are especially prominent in humans.

#9654. HEPA air purifiers may boost brain function after just one month

Adults aged 40 and older performed about 12% faster on a test of executive function after using a HEPA air purifier at home for one month. The findings suggest that cleaner indoor air could offer a simple way to help protect the brain from the effects of traffic-related pollution.

#9655. Scientists challenge a 70-year-old “lizard brain” myth

A long-standing picture of brain evolution as newer, rational layers stacked on top of an ancient “lizard brain” may be deeply misleading. Researchers found that evolution instead appears to balance two competing styles of brain wiring, expanding one while shrinking the other depending on what an animal needs to survive.

#9656. Too much or too little sleep may make your body age faster

Both too little and too much sleep may be linked to faster aging throughout the body. Researchers analyzing 23 biological aging clocks found the lowest aging levels among people sleeping about 6.4 to 7.8 hours per day. Short or long sleep was also associated with numerous conditions affecting the brain, heart, lungs, metabolism, and digestive system.

#9657. Childhood trauma may leave a lasting “scar” inside brain cells

Early-life stress may physically alter how DNA is packaged in the brain, leaving stress-related genes primed to activate more easily years later. In mice, researchers were able to block this effect, preventing the heightened anxiety and stress sensitivity that otherwise appeared in adulthood.

#9658. Your brain may not actually “make” decisions

A neuroscientist is challenging the idea that the brain contains a central system that actually “makes” our decisions. What we experience as choosing may instead emerge from the continuous interaction of perception, movement, the body, and the world around us.

#9659. Depression may shut down the brain’s ability to make new neurons

A major study found that adults with depression show disrupted production of new neurons in the hippocampus, potentially weakening the brain’s ability to separate new experiences from painful memories. The researchers also identified broad molecular changes that could open the door to new treatments tailored to different biological forms of depression.

#9660. A major Alzheimer’s risk gene may shrink brain cells years before symptoms

Researchers have uncovered a possible reason why the Alzheimer’s risk gene APOE4 can disrupt the brain long before memory problems begin. In mice, APOE4 increased a protein called Nell2, shrinking neurons and making memory circuits unusually active. That early hyperactivity predicted worse memory later in life. Reducing Nell2 in adult mice reversed the abnormal changes, raising hopes for a new way to intervene before Alzheimer’s progresses.

#9661. Schizophrenia’s lost brain connections follow a surprising pattern

Specialized brain scans reveal that schizophrenia is linked to widespread loss of the synapses that connect brain cells, with the left side of the brain hit especially hard. The damage follows a surprisingly organized pattern tied to the brain’s chemistry and wiring. Researchers also identified a left frontal region that may serve as a starting point for the loss.

#9662. Scientists reveal how the brain changes its connections when uncertainty strikes

The brain has a remarkably flexible system for handling uncertainty and changing situations. Researchers found that the frontoparietal cortex constantly shifts how it communicates with other brain regions depending on what information is needed to make a decision. Rather than simply becoming more active when things get difficult, this network appears to reorganize itself in real time.

#9663. A therapy that “rewrites” childhood memories can ease fear of failure

Childhood criticism can leave people carrying a fear of failure long into adulthood, but new research suggests those emotional patterns may be surprisingly flexible. Young adults who revisited painful memories through imagery-based therapy showed lasting reductions in fear, guilt, sadness, and stress.

#9664. A little-known protein may be fueling Alzheimer’s — and scientists found a way to block it

An experimental Alzheimer’s compound prevented damaging protein clumps from forming in the brains of mice, helping nerve cells survive longer and reducing amyloid buildup. The treatment also appeared to improve heart health and slow some signs of aging, raising hopes for an entirely new way to combat dementia.

#9665. A stress hormone may help the brain repair itself

A surprising stress-related signal may help the brain repair itself after injury. Researchers found that myelin-producing precursor cells rapidly release the stress hormone CRH near damaged brain tissue, helping control how those cells mature and rebuild protective nerve insulation. The same system also influences brain development and the thickness of myelin later in life. The findings could offer new clues about how early-life stress contributes to psychiatric disorders.

#9666. Scientists discover a hidden brain rhythm that could improve Parkinson’s treatment

Scientists have pinpointed a brain network—and its distinctive electrical rhythm—that appears to drive the benefits of deep brain stimulation for Parkinson’s disease. The discovery could lead to more precise, personalized stimulation settings and better results for patients.

#9759. How to Stay Informed But Not Overwhelmed by Election News

You pick up your phone to check one thing—perhaps the weather, a text message, or the score of a game. A few minutes later, you have read three election headlines, watched a clip from a debate, skimmed dozens of comments, and somehow learned that democracy, the economy, your health care, and the future of the planet may all be in danger before breakfast. If this sounds familiar, you are not alone. Election season can make the news feel unusually personal. The issues on the ballot can affect our finances, health, schools, communities, and rights. At the same time, the way we now encounter political information—constantly, emotionally, and often in fragments—can leave us feeling overloaded rather than informed. There is even a name for this experience: election stress. The Mayo Clinic describes it as the overwhelming anxiety some people feel during an election cycle. And surveys from the American Psychological Association suggest that elections have become a significant source of stress for many Americans. In the lead-up to the 2024 presidential election, for example, 72% of adults said they were worried that the results could lead to violence, and 56% said they feared the election could mark the end of democracy in the United States. It is understandable to feel unsettled when an election seems to carry serious consequences. But anxiety is not always a problem to get rid of; in small doses, it can direct our attention toward what matters. It may motivate us to learn, vote, volunteer, have difficult conversations, or support people affected by political decisions. The problem begins when information meant to help us engage instead keeps our nervous systems in a constant state of alarm. Why elections can feel so stressful Part of election stress comes from the uncertainty it elicits. We do not know who will win, what policies will change, or how the people around us will respond. Research suggests that uncertainty itself can be stressful because people naturally want to understand and predict the environment around them. When we feel unable to do that, we may feel less safe and less in control. News can intensify that uncertainty. Negative news is often more attention-grabbing than positive news, and conflict, danger, and outrage make especially compelling headlines. That does not mean journalists are intentionally trying to harm us. Reporting on threats and problems is an essential part of journalism. But a steady diet of alarming information can shape how we feel about the world, especially when it leaves little room for context, solutions, or signs of human cooperation. This can create a difficult cycle. When people feel anxious, they often seek out more information in an effort to regain a sense of control. Yet the more election news they encounter—particularly news marked by uncertainty, conflict, and emotional intensity—the more anxious they may feel. Then they check again. In other words, we may turn to the news to calm ourselves down, only to leave feeling more activated than before. Why today’s media environment makes it harder Political stress is not new. But the way we receive political information has changed dramatically. When John F. Kennedy and Richard Nixon debated on television in 1960, viewers watched in a far more contained media environment. There was no endless stream of clips, reactions, memes, push notifications, and commentary arriving at every hour of the day. People could discuss the debate afterward, but they were not also carrying the debate around in their pockets. Today, election news can follow us almost everywhere: to the grocery store line, the waiting room, the car, the dinner table, and bed. Social media makes it easier for people to participate politically and amplify voices that were once excluded from public conversation. Those are meaningful benefits. But the same technology can make political information feel impossible to put down. It also encourages us to consume news in small, emotionally potent pieces. A headline while brushing our teeth. A video clip while waiting for coffee. A dramatic post at a stoplight. These snippets can provoke strong feelings without giving us enough context to understand the story fully. That matters because headlines often tell us what to feel before they tell us what happened. Pew research found that five out of eight Americans (63%) said they only read news headlines rather than full news articles, specifically when scrolling through social media. When people repeatedly take in emotionally charged fragments of news, they may feel a growing sense of threat while having less detailed information to guide their judgments. When stress turns into news avoidance Faced with this constant emotional demand, some people keep checking the news compulsively. Others decide they have had enough and stop paying attention altogether. News avoidance can offer temporary relief. If every update makes you tense, angry, or helpless, closing the app may feel like self-protection. In fact, a report from the American Press Institute found that a third of Americans want to disengage from news because it feels stressful and overwhelming. But avoiding news altogether comes with costs, especially during an election year. It can make it harder to participate in democratic life, understand issues that affect our communities, or distinguish reliable reporting from misinformation passed along by friends, influencers, or highly partisan accounts. The goal, then, is not to consume more news or no news. It is to develop a healthier relationship with news: one that helps us stay informed without asking our nervous systems to be on call all day. Three ways to stay informed—and calm 1. Choose a news source that includes solutions, not only problems. During stressful times, it can help to seek out reporting that does more than identify what is going wrong. Constructive journalism still covers serious problems, but it also provides context, examines solutions, and asks what people and communities can do next. This approach is not about pretending everything is fine. It is about giving readers a more complete picture: What is the problem? Who is affected? What is being tried? What can citizens realistically do? Solution-oriented reporting may be especially helpful during elections because it can replace helplessness with a more grounded sense of agency and hope for the future. When you choose a news source, consider asking: Does this reporting leave me better informed? Does it help me understand the issue, or does it only make me feel alarmed? Does it explain possible solutions as well as the threat? 2. Balance distressing news with inspiring media. We do not need to force ourselves to be positive about difficult realities. But we can balance the emotional weight of election coverage with media that remind us of human goodness, beauty, courage, and connection. Research suggests that stories of kindness, moral courage, and collective care can evoke feelings such as elevation, gratitude, and hope. These emotions can broaden our view of what is possible and help us feel more connected to other people. And new research from the University of Santa Barbara suggests that these emotions can even reduce stress. This might mean watching a short video about neighbors helping one another after a disaster, reading a profile of local organizers working across differences, listening to music that helps you settle, or returning to a film or show that leaves you feeling more hopeful about people, or nostalgic about the past. Think of this as building a more balanced media diet. Election news can be an important part of that diet, but it should not be the only thing we feed our minds. Just as eating one healthy meal a week does not transform our physical health, one inspiring story will not undo chronic stress. Over time, though, regularly choosing media that restore us, physically and mentally, can help cultivate resilience, hope, and perspective. 3. Create boundaries around when you check the news. Finally, consider treating news less like a snack and more like a meal. Snacking on news all day can mean consuming a lot without ever feeling nourished. You absorb a headline here, an alert there, and a few comments in between—but never get enough context to make sense of what you are seeing. The result can be a lot of emotional calories and very little understanding. Instead, choose one or two intentional times to engage with the news. You might read a full article over breakfast, listen to a trusted news program on your commute, or set aside 15 minutes at lunch to review the day’s major stories. If you are prone to checking headlines compulsively, digital wellness strategies such as app limits or notification settings can make those boundaries easier to keep. It may also help to avoid making late-night news “snacking” part of your bedtime routine. By the end of the day, our emotional and cognitive resources are often already depleted. A stream of alarming headlines can quickly turn into doomscrolling: the repeated consumption of negative online content that leaves us more agitated and less able to sleep. Giving news its own place in the day does not mean becoming indifferent. It means making room to take in information carefully, in context, and with enough emotional space to respond thoughtfully. Staying engaged without staying alarmed Election stress is real because elections matter. Feeling concerned about the future does not mean that something is wrong with you; it may mean you are paying attention. But paying attention does not require constant exposure to distress. We can choose reporting that informs and empowers us, balance difficult coverage with media that reminds us of human possibility, and set boundaries that give our minds a chance to rest. The aim is not emotional numbness. It is becoming the kind of informed citizen who can face difficult news, stay connected to what matters, and still have enough calm and hope left to act.

#9760. Ten TV Series That Highlight the Best in Humanity: 2026

Every year, we invite staff and regular contributors to nominate TV series for Greater Goodies, the awards we give to stories that exemplify or illustrate kindness, community, empathy, and more keys to well-being. Here’s what we came up with this year: The Boroughs, Death by Lightning, Envidiosa, The Hawk, Heated Rivalry, A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms, Lucky, Murderbot, Pluribus, and Shrinking. The Aging-as-a-Virtue Award: The Boroughs (Netflix) Name the last show you’ve seen that features a hero over the age of 60. Drawing a blank? It’s not your fault—the media’s modus operandi is to equate heroism with youthfulness. Netflix’s The Boroughs flips this stereotype on its head. With a star-studded lead cast, all 70 or older, we follow Sam Cooper’s (Alfred Molina) experience as he reluctantly moves into a senior living community in New Mexico after the death of his wife. But a series of strange events suggests that this is no ordinary retirement community. As Sam navigates grief, new friendships, and a new identity, he joins forces with his neighbors to solve and resolve the community mystery. Overcoming obstacles like age discrimination and self-doubts, the group peels back the layers of the strange occurrences. At the root of the mystery is a societal fear of aging. The show highlights what researcher Margie Lachman calls “crystallized intelligence”: the culmination of life experience, memory, and know-how. In The Boroughs, that turns out to be just as heroic as super human strength or the ability to fly. — Shanna B. TiayonThe Purpose Award: Death by Lightning (Netflix) History has already told us how Death by Lightning ends: Charles Guiteau will assassinate President James Garfield in 1881. But why? That’s one of the questions explored by the series, and it finds motivation in a sense of purpose–how purpose forms and how people come to cross-purposes. Garfield (Michael Shannon) is principled and drawn reluctantly into a presidential contest he entered to nominate someone else. Guiteau (Matthew Macfadyen), meanwhile, is disturbingly sympathetic: charming, articulate, and desperate to do something meaningful with his life. Despite failed ventures, including a stint at the Oneida free-love commune, he remains convinced greatness awaits him. Believing his campaigning secured Garfield’s victory, he expects a government position to confirm his worth. As their stories converge, Garfield finds purpose in his presidency: fighting corruption while supporting education and equal opportunity. Guiteau pursues a reward he feels he deserves, an entitlement that leads to tragedy. In these ways, Garfield and Guiteau offer sharply contrasting answers to what makes a life meaningful, and watching their stories unfold side by side, we see how differently the desire to matter can play out. Who you really ought to be watching closely is Garfield’s Vice President, Chester A. Arthur (Nick Offerman). A comfortable beneficiary of the political machine, he becomes an unlikely source of hope as the story goes on. Offerman makes him funny, flawed, and increasingly affecting as the demands of office begin to collide with old loyalties. In a political landscape that can leave us cynical–in both the 19th century and the 21st–the series makes room for something more hopeful: the possibility that even an unlikely person can rise to the occasion, if they know why they need to rise. – Aurelia Santos The Empathy Award: Envidiosa (Netflix) When almost-40-year-old Vicky (Griselda Siciliani) breaks up with her long-term boyfriend, she is driven to embark upon a journey of self-discovery. At first, Vicky is just looking for a replacement for her boyfriend—but the problem is that her compulsive comparisons with other women sabotage attempts to connect with other people. (Indeed, the name of this Argentinian show, Envidiosa, means “envious.”) Vicky is self-absorbed and yet at the same time she doesn’t have a strong sense of her own self. We see this vividly in her hilarious sessions with her therapist. When, for example, she goes on an envious rant about a friend’s milestone, she is forced to confront the quiet, uncomfortable realization that she never once asked how her friend actually felt. In short, Vicky is not a polished role model—and over the course of four seasons, we see her empathy grow through the painful process of unlearning self-centeredness. Personally, I see Envidiosa as an opportunity to question how we show up for others’ joys and struggles, and I take it as an invitation to reflect: How can I empathize with the ups and downs of those around me? Envidiosa is a funny and at times embarrassingly relatable story about trying to become a more empathic human being and friend. — Criss Cuervo The Zest-for-Life Award: The Hawk (Netflix) In The Hawk, washed-up professional golfer Lonnie “The Hawk” Hawkins (Will Ferrell) has made a miracle comeback into the PGA tour—this time around, facing a tough competitor in his son, Lance (Jimmy Tatro). It’s a screwball comedy, full of dirty jokes and a rapid-fire rapport between Lonnie and his not-quite-ex-wife, Stacy (Molly Shannon). A makeshift-caddy he “picked up at Walmart” (while chatting up the parking lot, from his camper van) adds to the fun, serving up an unlikely duo that seem happy just to be alive. Even when he’s pretty down and out, Lonnie is looking on the bright side of things. Everything is done with gusto—he chats up the golf ball before each swing, he devours plates of Buffalo Wild Wings, he plays mini golf with his son and makes it a full-on competition. “Son, never play golf angry,” he tells Lance. For Lonnie, life is all about golf—and golf is all about fun. Why spend all your time on something if you aren’t doing it for the joy of it? I especially love Lonnie’s adoration for his estranged wife, a firecracker in her own right. In episode four, when the Hawk crashes his son’s engagement party–yes, you read that right–and then attempts a getaway with his caddie in the van, Stacy hurls the cake at them, along with a string of expletives. It doesn’t even faze him. “You gotta love the passion!” Lonnie gushes. “That is one hot tamale!” — Hope Reese The Bridging Differences Award: Heated Rivalry Heated Rivalry follows stick-crossed lovers Shane and Ilya as they figure out how to be intimate within the highly competitive, homophobic environment of professional hockey. At first, Shane and Ilya swap fluids more readily than emotions. But over the course of six episodes, the two learn to love across gaps in culture, language, and experience. In one pivotal scene, Shane invites Ilya to speak vulnerably about family dynamics in Ilya’s native Russian—despite not understanding a word of it. That works because Shane does not focus attention on himself. Instead, he stays present on the phone, serving as a witness to Ilya’s visceral emotions. Their connection lies in felt care, in hearing the texture of another’s heartbreak.  In similar fashion, Ilya grounds Shane in stressful encounters. He supports Shane through panic without wallowing in his lover’s fear and entices Shane to relish pleasures outside of straight standards. As a couple, they help each other explore complex parts of themselves. We see a similar process unfold in another couple in the show, as they learn to respect each other’s different needs. People might tune into Heated Rivalry for locker room towel drops and HBO’s glossy cinematography. They should stay to see a couple learn to love each other’s differences. The show exemplifies how bridging differences, at its best, means supporting others to more fully encounter themselves, and how in the process our relationships can grow. With plenty of additional homoerotic stick-and-ball games to pull on from the novels of Rachel Reid, the next season of Heated Rivalry is expected to air on HBO Max and Canada’s Crave in Spring 2027. — Luke BrownThe Integrity Award: A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms (HBO) The monstrously successful HBO series Game of Thrones famously tossed out a lot of good-and-evil fantasy tropes to tell a morally complicated story of how power corrupts even the best people. A prequel called House of the Dragon shares that worldview. That’s why a new prequel called A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms came as something of a surprise to fans. Unlike the other two series, this is not a story about a struggle for power. Instead, it focuses on a rather naive, not-too-bright “hedge knight” named Duncan (Peter Claffey) seeking to be hired by whatever lord will take him. Though Duncan is taller and stronger than most, he’s touchingly childlike as he tries to find his place in the merry old kingdoms of Westeros. The first two episodes are shockingly funny and charming, as Duncan acquires a squire in the form of a little bald boy going by the name Egg (Dexter Sol Ansell), and fumblingly tries to enter a jousting tournament. And then Duncan finds himself on a collision course with the great and powerful of Westeros. When violence finally comes, there’s nothing casual about it. We feel the force of every blow, the tragedy of every death, and the erosion of Duncan’s innocence. I’m giving A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms the award for integrity, but I can’t tell you why without spoiling the ending. Let’s just say that the series turns out to be the story of how one simple young man discovers what is most important to him—and finds the courage to stick to that path even in the face of isolation, temptations, and threats. — Jeremy Adam Smith The Redemption Award: Lucky (Apply TV)  How do you exit a life you were born into?   That’s the question behind Lucky, starring Anya Taylor-Joy. Lucky was raised by her con-artist father and taught the “family business” from a young age—but when we meet her, she navigating internal conflict with the life she’s been told to live and the life she wants for herself. Her desire to quit is complicated by her marriage to Cary, the son of a ruthless mob leader, who was also socialized into a life of crime. The couple plan one final con as their exit strategy, but it goes terribly wrong. Lucky sets out to resolve the botched plan, reconcile her marriage, and protect both her life and her father’s. Her intelligence is put to the test as she puts the pieces together, connects the dots, and outsmarts her rivals. In the process she discovers her own virtue—and that the intelligence which made her a successful con artist can also set her free. Through the course of the series, she realizes that she does not have to pay for the sins of her father. Having never been given the opportunity for self-determination, she makes peace in the best way she knows how and chooses a new path. — Shanna B. TiayonThe Belonging Award: Murderbot (Apple TV) The Murderbot Diaries are a series of science-fiction novellas by Martha Wells, about a genderless, robotic soldier who manages to achieve free will. Since discovery will result in deactivation and since it doesn’t like humans very much anyway, Murderbot conceals its freedom of mind and consumes a lot of human-made entertainment inside its own head, even as it externally follows every order given to it by the evil corporation that owns it. Murderbot even has a favorite TV show: the Star-Trek-like Rise and Fall of Sanctuary Moon. As a result of this isolation, the novellas are built almost entirely around its memorable inner monologues, with a voice that is by turns snarky, vulnerable, cold-blooded, disgusted, and puzzled. One of the things that makes these books distinctive is that Murderbot is not Pinocchio—it has no wish to become a “real boy.” It doesn’t want a gender, or the same feelings and drives as people. It just wants to be itself—and to have that difference respected. While the Apple TV show based on the books is not quite that original or interesting, Murderbot retains its focus on finding the balance between belonging and difference. When we meet Murderbot (Alexander Skarsgård), he’s been hired to protect a group of polyamorous hippie scientists who hail from a utopian colony that exists independently of the corporations that rule the rest of the galaxy. They are Murderbot’s first encounter with humans not shaped by dehumanizing hyper-capitalistic values, and they observe Murderbot in a way the corporate humans cannot. Over the course of the first season, they begin to realize that there is more to Murderbot than meets the eye, and they learn to see and appreciate Murderbot on its own terms. But what’s crucial is not that they perceive the humanity in Murderbot—it’s that Murderbot sees the humanity (and the goodness) in them. In time, it becomes an accepted part of their society—without becoming more like them. Even so, the first season concludes on a tough-minded, melancholy note, as Murderbot itself can’t quite feel like it belongs. Will it someday find a place among these crunchy idealists? Stay tuned: The second season is coming in 2027. — Jeremy Adam Smith The Difficult-Human-Appreciation Award: Pluribus (Apple TV) Have you ever been among people but you didn’t feel part of the crowd? Like they were in on a joke that you just couldn’t get? Welcome to Carol Sturka’s world. The science-fiction premise of Pluribus is that an alien virus transforms humanity into one big happy hive mind—everyone except Carol and 13 other people who seem to have immunity. Even among those remaining individuals, though, Carol stands out as a malcontent. We sense that she’s been a fish out of water for her whole life. Indeed, not every viewer will find Carol relatable or likable. When we first meet her, she’s at a bookstore event promoting her highly successful romantasy series. With her fans, she’s charming, funny, and kind. When she gets into a limo after a big sendoff by readers, the drivers asks if she’s famous. “That depends,” Carol replies. “Are you a big fan of mindless crap?” In short, Carol hates herself, her work, and her readers. And as we discover, her personality is snarky, uptight, stubborn, and uncompromising. When the virus hits, it’s an apocalyptic event, but Carol doesn’t change one bit—and her traits become both a blessing and a curse. The whole world wants to be kind to her, but Carol can’t accept kindness. That drives her to fight back. And that’s what ultimately makes her the hero of the story. In the end, Pluribus is a very Greater Good thought experiment. It takes prosociality to its limits and finds that a total togetherness, empathy, and cooperation to the exclusion of selfishness, crankiness, and boundaries is a self-destructive dystopia, not a utopia. The show seems to argue that humanity needs difficult people like Carol to survive, and so invites us to appreciate what the Carols of the world have to offer. — Jeremy Adam Smith The Deep Listening Award: Shrinking (AppleTV) In Shrinking, talking and listening are the name of the game. Jimmy (Jason Segel) is a widowed psychotherapist who helps patients navigate anxiety, OCD, PTSD, and other ailments as he himself is floundering. But he’s not alone. His teenage daughter, overbearing-but-lovable neighbors, and coworker-friends (with Harrison Ford as the curmudgeonly head of the practice) are all there to help him when his life goes off the rails after his wife was killed by a drunk driver. In its three-season run, the series is heavy-handed with its use of therapy-speak among the whole gang, whether between therapist-to-patient, therapist-to-daughter, or therapist-to-therapist. Characters “feel seen.” More times than I can count, they tell each other “I hear you.” We witness, in detail, exchanges inside the therapy office—and outside of it, in a rogue practice known as “Jimmying,” where therapy takes place at such venues as, for instance, the boxing ring. In other words, there’s a lot of “deep listening”: the practice of being present, open, and empathic with another person, in a way that goes beyond just hearing and understanding words. In his grief, Jimmy himself is not always a perfect deep listener. He tends to talk more than a typical therapist, and frequently injects himself into the discussion. Shrinking isn’t about perfection—it’s about struggling to connect and growing in the process. – Hope Reese

#9761. What a Fictional Robot Can Teach Us About Care

This essay contains mild spoilers for the novel Klara and the Sun. The baby, my baby, was to be named Josie. Josie Joy. But when she was born, she was decidedly not a Josie. The name was too soft, too ethereal for such a sentient, wide-awake, bird-like baby. I knew that I had to find a new name. Who are you? I whispered into the swirls of her astoundingly warm, circulated, and singularly perfect ear. I couldn’t wait to get to know her. In another story, a girl named Josie is dying. In Kazuo Ishiguro’s 2021 novel Klara and the Sun, Josie has an unknown illness and a prognosis that haunts the pages. Her life exists in the shadow of her sister, Sal, who has already died of the same unarticulated illness, likely caused by an elective genetic alteration procedure known as “lifting.” In Josie’s world, being lifted is the new social currency; those who are lifted are expected to have greater access to both intellectual and economic success. The procedure does not, however, come without risk. When we meet Josie’s mother, she is tasked with the impossible: coping with the impending death of her only remaining child. A death likely resulting from a choice she made as a parent. The ultimate consequence. Klara is a robot purchased by Josie’s mom to be her companion, in part (it is implied) to assuage the guilt she feels. I first read Ishiguro’s Klara and the Sun shortly after its debut. Despite being disturbed by some aspects of the dystopian world depicted within, my memory of the novel was poignant. I recalled Klara with particular affection. Now, the distance between Ishiguro’s dystopian world and our current reality is rapidly collapsing. With the much anticipated film adaptation set to be released this Friday, October 23, and the sudden ubiquity of artificial intelligence in our day-to-day lives, Klara and the Sun is now repositioned as a mirror. If we look closely enough, we might discern what remains uniquely human during this moment of profound technological and social change. Care through other eyes We are introduced to Klara, the story’s narrator, when Josie’s mom takes her to find an artificial friend, or AF for short. In the novel, AFs confer social standing, but also offer practical assistance—tutoring, companionship, monitoring an ailing child—not unlike the benefits purported by artificial intelligence companies today. Klara has been patiently waiting, alongside several other AFs, to be chosen. Josie’s mother is the final decider. In a foreboding scene, Josie’s mother commands Klara to reenact Josie’s gait—a test, of sorts—before agreeing to purchase her. It’s the precision of Klara’s mimicry that convinces “the Mother” (as she’s called in the novel) that Klara is the right model for Josie. All of this, we learn through Klara. Ishiguro’s choice to position the reader within Klara’s perspective allows us to bypass the quagmire question of whether or not Klara is conscious and instead project our own consciousness onto her. In doing so, we view the human world through her sometimes malfunctioning robot eyes, as she nonjudgmentally conveys the complexities of the very flawed and sometimes desperate human behaviors she seeks to understand. In one particularly moving scene, Klara witnesses the vulnerability of caring for another human being: I saw before me, on the near edge of the bed, a large shifting shape, made additionally complex by the patches of blackness and moonlight moving over its surface. I realized the shape was the Mother and Josie embracing. . . . As well as their limbs, their hair had become mingled, and then their shape began gently to rock, in a way not unlike when their goodbyes became extended. “Don’t want to die, Mom. I don’t want that.” “It’s okay. Okay.” The Mother’s voice was soft, at just the same level mine had been. “I don’t want that, Mom.” “I know. I know. It’s okay.” Prior to this moment, Klara—steadfast and ever-available—had offered her presence to comfort Josie. Yet, despite their far more complicated and at times strained relationship, it is the Mother that Josie calls for. Care creates love When I first became a parent, I confided to a friend that when my not-Josie was born, I felt like a new sun had burst into my solar system, and I was now orbiting around her. I couldn’t quite articulate the “before,” in my metaphor. I didn’t feel like I was previously my own sun, nor did I feel like I was a planet untethered to others. But there was something distinctly different when she arrived, a whole new consciousness, a space and force and awareness created within me. Her pull was intoxicating, undeniable, sometimes terrifying, and my awareness and attention seemed to expand overnight to now include not only my own experiences, but also to track, perceive, anticipate, and accommodate her own. If Klara had turned to psychological theory and research about the complexity of parent-child bonds, she would have found a great deal of it. Consider Winnicott’s “good-enough mother” or research indicating that parents and infants are rarely, if ever, perfectly attuned, and the growing body of evidence suggesting that the navigation of the ruptures and repairs themselves reinforce attachment. It has become increasingly clear through evolving applications of experiments such as Edward Tronick’s now famous “still-face” experiment that the imperfect dance between caregiver and child lays the groundwork for resilience and emotional regulation. In The Philosophical Baby, developmental psychologist and philosopher Alison Gopnik says, “It’s not so much that we care for children because we love them, as that we love them because we care for them.” We love them because we care for them. Beyond Gopnik’s own research, there is an abundance of data to back this up. Studies I have long been familiar with, such as those demonstrating that in same-sex, non-carrying male couples, fathers who act as primary parents show changes in similar brain regions to women who carried their pregnancies and go on to parent their children. Yet, there was something profound about the simplicity of Gopnik’s statement, about love being something we cultivate within ourselves and, more precisely, about care being the means through which we build love, not merely a necessary byproduct. In a separate interview, Gopnik jokes that spending time with a three-year-old gives you a small taste of sainthood. Not in the sense that the demands are so extreme that you inherently become a saint (though there is a real argument to be made here for any parent who weathers the storm of a parking lot tantrum), but in the actual felt-sense of the divine, the experience of feeling so acutely and utterly in love with this being, so in awe of their singularity, their specialness, their openness to experience, their brilliance. Gopnik continues on, teasing, about our tendency to claim the uniqueness of our three-year-old, in her case her grandchild, but gently affirms the truth beneath: that there is something numinous, universal, pure within all three-year-olds, a near-magical vitality that we sometimes make contact with when we intimately care for one. Something primal, or perhaps primary, is awoken when we re-experience the world through the eyes of a three-year-old. Care and distance My not-Josie is now three. In many ways she is more unknowable, separate, than she was when she was a newborn, so physically attached and dependent on me. Now, she has her own relationships—with teachers, classmates, family members—that are entirely her own. And yet, she occupies and orients so much of me, continually changing my experience of the world, my access to play, to awe, to unadulterated, intoxicating adoration.  This past summer, I was sitting by a lake, relaxed but hawk-eyed as I watched my not-Josie from the shore. I felt a familiar presence by my side, the barely-perceptible shadow in my peripheral vision of a small body sitting down, and soon thereafter, the unselfconscious weight of a small hand on my leg. I looked down to see a head covered in feathery blonde hair, not the dark, curly brown of my own child. The wispy tufts belonged to another small child who had clearly mistaken me for her mother. In that brief moment before she looked up, before we both acknowledged our separateness, I felt overcome with the awareness—as though received through the touch of her hand on my leg—that her mother loved her in the way that I love my child, and that, despite how unbelievably special my not-Josie is, my love for her is not. It is precious, but also universal. Intellectually, of course, this is not a profound realization. Emotionally touching into it is, on the other hand, equally as brutal as it is beautiful. Next to this small child, healthy and safe, I felt her preciousness, but also that of all the children flooding my news feed every day who are not healthy and safe, and whose parents must live with this love and that devastation. Care and consciousness On the back cover, Klara and the Sun is described as a story about love, which it undeniably is. But I think it’s perhaps more fundamentally about care. Care is arguably more tangible than love, but also more complex, friction-prone, messy. It’s the gaps, the mistakes, the harms, the repairs, the precarity, the forgiving, that make care so deeply painful and so deeply nourishing. Nourishing enough to sustain the vulnerability of love. It is care that drives Josie’s mother to have her “lifted” in an age-old attempt of desperation to give her child what she perceives as the best chance at a full life in a rapidly reorganizing world. It is care that sends the Mother down the path of trying to find a way to have Klara “continue” Josie’s life, even though she has already tried and failed to recreate her first child’s consciousness in an artificial intelligence through the work of an artist and scientist named Capaldi. It is care that allows the Mother—despite her cold exterior—to fearlessly comfort her child as she confronts the possibility of her own death, enveloping her, rocking her, soothing her through the unimaginable. It is the Mother’s human care, not her cowardice, that makes her love true. There is so much in Klara and the Sun that is undeniably relevant in this particular social, cultural, and technological moment, so many ways in which Ishiguro’s Josie and my not-Josie’s world are suddenly uncomfortably proximate, but it is what Klara herself affirms about humanity that is most resonant. At the end of the novel, Klara reflects on what she’s learned. She did not have to become a Josie 2.0, for Josie survived. Klara is glad for this, of course, believing that it was her bargain with the Sun (which she sees as a kind of god) that saved Josie. Sitting discarded in a junk yard, Klara is increasingly convinced that the desperate plan to turn Josie into a digital consciousness wouldn’t have worked: Mr. Capaldi believed there was nothing special inside Josie that couldn’t be continued. He told the Mother he’d searched and searched and found nothing like that. But I believe now he was searching in the wrong place. There was something very special, but it wasn’t inside Josie. It was inside those who loved her. That’s why I think now Mr. Capaldi was wrong and I wouldn’t have succeeded. It is what happens within us and between us when we care for another being that differentiates us as humans. In other words, it’s not so much what’s there but what’s not there—the space between the person in front of us and how we perceive, imperfectly care for, and ultimately love them—that makes us human. Through Klara’s eyes, I was able to see more clearly what connects the Mother and her Josie to me and my not-Josie. It is the very fact that we can so wholeheartedly love another being, without being them, having them, solving them, knowing every bit of them. It is the fact that still, we carry them in our hearts, and somehow we are not heavier for it.

#9762. Should We Look to Hunter-Gatherer Societies as Models of Equality?

On the shores of Lake Eyasi in Northern Tanzania, a small number of people still hunt and gather their food. Today a Hadza hunter has taken down an animal—a kudu. He can’t carry it by himself, so he cuts his poisoned arrow from its flank, covers the carcass in dry grass to hide it from hyenas, and heads home to enlist help. News spreads like wildfire across the camp. Everybody rushes to the kill. They divide up the carcass, carry it back, then divvy it up some more. Every family gets some, and after the meat is cooked, there’s more sharing still. On a good day like today, the hunter and his family retain half, but sometimes they keep much less. People may even walk from other camps, and almost everyone, from miles around, is entitled to a share. The post-hunt scene above is idyllic. It paints a quintessential picture of egalitarianism and equality. But our experience as anthropologists working with the Hadza people of Tanzania and with the Tsimane people of Bolivia’s Amazon rainforest is that inequalities are part of even the most equal of societies. Furthermore, the equalities that do exist often emerge from selfish motives. Insights like ours are revising researchers’ understanding of both the past and how people operate today, as inequality grows globally. Hunter-gatherers are often at the center of heated academic battles about what it means to be human, including debates over gender, war, and peace and whether humans are, by nature, egalitarian or hierarchical. Sometimes these debates oversimplify. So we pulled together hundreds of different sources covering more than a century of ethnography to find out just how egalitarian hunter-gatherer and other subsistence societies really are.         People living in egalitarian societies like the Tsimane have similar, modest levels of material wealth (Christopher von Rueden).               Is any society truly egalitarian? Anthropologists have called hunter-gatherers such as the Hadza “egalitarian societies,” with equalities of power, wealth, and social status. And it’s true that the traditional subsistence communities we work with successfully limit many inequalities. Most families have similar, modest possessions and store little material wealth. When leaders do emerge, they are highly constrained in how they can direct other people. People often make decisions via consensus, and, if they cannot reach agreement, families will move away. While the same anthropologists also emphasize that some hunter-gatherers are very unequal and that equality takes deliberate effort, the prevailing impression is that equality characterizes hunter-gatherers, past and present. A quick trip to your local bookshop will reveal grand claims, including that past hunter-gatherer societies had no permanent leadership and no hierarchy, inequality only spread with the development of agriculture, and humans are noble by nature. Some scientists are pushing back against these utopian portrayals. The popular bestseller, The Dawn of Everything, calls egalitarianism a “Garden of Eden narrative” that lacks substance. Other scholars have called egalitarian societies a scientific origin myth. So where does the truth lie? Probably somewhere in between. Inequality among hunter-gatherers In our recent wide-ranging review of the evidence from the early 20th century onward, we found that no society lacked inequality. Like the Hadza, the Ju/’Hoansi of the Kalahari desert in southern Africa had little property and widespread food-sharing, yet certain families controlled valuable hunting territory surrounding waterholes. The Bayaka of the central African rainforest had little material inequality, yet they bought, sold, and hoarded ritual knowledge. The Philippine Agta controlled and arranged their children’s marriages, hugely discriminating against orphans. Gender inequalities were commonplace. The Tanzanian Hadza keep certain cuts of meat exclusively for older men, and give more respect to better hunters. Across societies, women have universally done more direct parenting, and men have participated more frequently in community-level decision making. No society lacks informal leadership. Age was another source of inequality. In many Australian societies, older men controlled ritual secrets and had power over the marriage choices of younger relatives. Equality arises from self-interest Our study also found that although hunter-gatherers are frequently generous, the processes limiting inequality often do not require any generosity. Instead, equality often results from people’s self-interested motives to acquire resources, status, and autonomy. A great example of this is food-sharing. Although each Hadza family gets a cut of kudu meat, it often happens not because the hunter’s family prioritizes total equality, but because other families request it vociferously. It can involve more taking than giving, often through strongly stated demands or complaints. Another example is ridicule and mockery of stingy or braggadocious individuals, which keeps greed and bravado in check. Paradoxically, status-seeking can also promote equality, when cooperating to gain status diffuses status throughout the community.         The Tsimane reach decisions through community deliberation. Some have more influential voices than others, but everyone has the opportunity to contribute (Christopher von Rueden).               Egalitarianism is alive and well Many of these inequality-reducing processes are familiar to people everywhere. Ridicule and mockery are ubiquitous in industrialized societies, from political cartoons to social media. Lampooning authority is even common in autocratic societies where governments spend large sums of money suppressing speech. Demands and complaints form the basis of strikes and collective bargaining. And key to social mobility is cooperation among people of different social strata. Like many great debates, the truth is complex: Our ancient hunter-gatherer ancestors didn’t reach the giddy heights of inequality seen in other societies over the last 6,000 years, but putting hunter-gatherers on a pedestal as paragons of equality is a mistake. Sweeping statements about equality in the past blind us to the tools that probably allowed ancient hunter-gatherers to combat inequality, just as they and people in all societies do today. Egalitarianism, then, is best seen not as perfect equality but as a set of tools that people use to secure their own autonomy and access to resources. Equality along one dimension or another might be the outcome, but it doesn’t rely on people being amazingly noble or completely lacking in greed or status-seeking. If you consider egalitarianism as equality-creating processes, you can see that it is a widespread reality among hunter-gatherer societies, and beyond. This article is republished from The Conversation under a Creative Commons license. Read the original article.

#9763. How Do You Raise Kids to Be Emotionally Mature?

Raising a teen is one of the last chapters in parenting. During these years, it’s easy to prioritize your child’s grades, sports, and other accolades, especially as they inch toward leaving the nest. While achievement is one marker of success, there’s another (often overlooked) skill that shapes your teen’s well-being and development: emotional maturity. According to clinical psychologist Lindsay Gibson, emotional maturity develops through deep emotional connections, beginning with a child’s parents and primary caretakers. Research shows emotional maturity is the ability to accept and express care, temper big feelings, and make wise choices. Without it, teens are more likely to feel lonely and less satisfied with life, one study found. In her latest book, How to Raise an Emotionally Mature Child, Gibson points out that “the quality of humanity’s future depends on the emotional maturity of coming generations.” Juli Fraga: In your book, you mention that humanity’s future will depend on emotional maturity. Why does this matter so much right now? Lindsay Gibson: Emotional maturity is critical to our well-being as individuals and to the smooth, productive, and innovative aspects of society. When you’re emotionally mature, you can think and anticipate consequences. You can also look back and learn from what you’ve done before. So, you can rely on your thinking to help you deal with reality. Emotional maturity also means tuning into your inner world, including your emotions, imagination, hopes, dreams, connections to other people, and sense of belonging. JF: Our society places a lot of emphasis on achievement, and less importance on emotional intelligence. How can parents help their teens balance the two? LG: There’s a real cultural pull in society to prize intelligence and achievement. But when parents get too fixated on these qualities, a child may feel like performance matters more than their thoughts, feelings, and sense of safety. Kids need to be safely held and supported through the growing-up process. It helps them feel resilient, secure, and confident—qualities they need to take out into the world to function independently. If you get too fixated on the intelligence and achievement parts, you’ve got someone who’s very powerful in one area, but whose emotional side may be underdeveloped. It’s the balance between the two that matters. JF: In your book, you mention why it’s so important for parents to help their kids develop a sense of self. Can you explain why this matters, especially during the teen years when kids are individuating? LG: One way teens find out who they are is by discovering who they’re not. Teens might push a limit or break a rule, and it’s all par for the course. What’s important for parents is to be able to recognize and imagine that their child has a separate heart and mind from theirs—a process called “mentalization.” A self is that inner spark that speaks to us. It says “don’t do that” or “now’s the time to go for it.” It guides you toward what’s most compatible with your individuality and who you are meant to be. Our sense of self comes from our relationships with people we love and who love us. To feel secure, kids need solid, dependable, connected, and deeply resonant relationships with their parents and caretakers. They need parents who are curious and reflect something back that’s meaningful about who the young person is. When they have that foundation, they have a sense of being a real person inside. JF: Parents are so busy these days, and overloaded—mentally and emotionally. Raising emotionally mature kids may feel like another big to-do on an already long list. What advice do you have? LG: Taking time to pause and take your child seriously as a real person will work wonders for their self-confidence and future resilience. Slowing down takes practice because by the time you’re old enough to have a family, you’ve probably been very goal-directed; parents can be pretty hard on themselves because our culture is so fixated on achievement. But pulling yourself away from your goal for even a second to tend to your child is something worth practicing because that’s what children’s needs are all about. Kids also develop confidence when they know they matter just as much as anybody else. JF: What are some behaviors that are normal for teens but that parents might misinterpret as pushback or misbehavior? LG: Sometimes teens experiment with saying or doing things that clash with their socialization up to that point, such as the language they use or the clothes they wear. These are things that the parent can feel like, “Whoa, I’m uncomfortable with this…” Here are two meaningful ways to navigate these moments. Ask your teen questions about what they’re into, such as the styles they like or what they admire about someone they idolize. Try showing interest, not intrusive interest, but just interest in what they like, or why they are experimenting with different things. Keep in mind that when your teen snaps at you, is in a bad mood, or slams the door, their emotional centers are activated in a way that will probably never be repeated in their life because their brains prioritize emotional development first during this stage. JF: Can you share more about why a teen’s development follows this trajectory? LG: Yeah, I know it’s easy to ask, “Why in the world would nature do it that way?” It’s because that’s how important the emotional self and relationships are to our real success as independent adults. As adults, we have to know ourselves inside and out and use our feelings to guide us. Teens are still trying to work this out. Learning how to handle these emotions takes a lot of practice. They’re finding out what it’s like to feel rage, or what it’s like to be in love, or to feel disappointed. And they’re feeling them in the most intense way they ever will, because the brain is taking on the job of developing them into a really deep, complete human being. JF: What advice do you have for parents when their teen’s emotions push their own buttons? LG: Keep in mind that when your teen responds in an adamant, emotional, defiant way, or calls you names, they’re not really in command of those emotions yet. Instead of coming down hard with words that suggest they’re not a good person, try to respond with: “I’m really interested in how you feel about this. I want to hear your arguments, but when you call me a name, it makes it impossible for me to be available to you. It just doesn’t work. We don’t call each other names in this family. That won’t work when you’re an adult, either.” The interaction then becomes an education about how people respond to different kinds of approaches. Harshness that stops a teen cold shuts down their impulse to communicate their feelings to other people, which isn’t good for resilience or honest relationships. JF: What are two things that parents do to foster emotional maturity that can actually backfire? LG: One is the importance of achievement, of having a good resume. Of course, parents know how hard life is as an adult. We feel it’s our duty to convey these realizations to our kids. But when it becomes something that feels like everything else will fall into place if you do these things first or achieve X, we are not helping our kids with the emotional or interpersonal side of life. We’re creating a one-dimensional kind of person. Sometimes parents also think that if they don’t give their child severe consequences or punishments, the child won’t learn and mature. When you try to convey information through punishment, you actually teach a child to defer to other people’s anger and control. Of course, we want them to be aware of consequences, but sometimes we can go too far. You can’t have a full, right life if you’re always anticipating that somebody might be mad at you.

#9764. What My Son Is Learning From Artificial Intelligence

My 10-year-old son came to me recently with a question about black holes. Not in the way children usually do, restless and easily redirected, but with the focused intensity of someone who had already been thinking for a while. He had been talking to an artificial intelligence—specifically, a large language model called Claude. I looked at their conversation. It had been good, I discovered. The explanations were patient and well-calibrated. He had followed up, pushed back, and received answers that adjusted to his level. By the time he found me, he was genuinely engaged, asking the kind of questions that suggest a mind that is curious and actively at work. But I have also been watching something else. It is not that he looks things up. Children with questions about black holes have always turned outward, to books or to a particularly knowledgable parent or teacher. What has changed is the speed. When the uncertainty is of a sort that once had to be lived with for a while, my son reaches for an answer sooner than he used to. The question is no longer something to sit with. It is something to resolve. And I find myself wondering what this means about his relationship to sitting with not knowing. That shift, subtle as it is, may point to something worth taking seriously. Losing productive friction I am a psychoanalyst, and I spend my professional life attending to what develops in the space between people, including what develops when that space is too quickly filled. Childhood has always involved productive friction. Not knowing how something works, waiting for an answer, trying to figure something out without immediate resolution. These experiences are not incidental to development, but are part of how the mind learns to tolerate uncertainty, to hold a question open, and to struggle toward understanding. The capacity to sit with not-knowing is itself a developmental achievement that underlies creative thinking, emotional regulation, and the ability to navigate a world that rarely resolves cleanly. I watched this in miniature not long ago. My son was waiting for a package—a computer part he had saved for—and the delivery had been delayed by weather. In an earlier childhood, mine perhaps, this would have been a long afternoon of checking the window. Instead he opened a chat to resolve the uncertainty: how long do weather delays usually last, what determines them, how do you read the tracking data from the delivery company to get the most accurate picture. The conversation was detailed and as far as I could tell the information was reliable. He came away with a small expertise in logistics, but with an estimate that turned out, in the end, to have no relation to anything. The package arrived that evening, on none of the schedules he had so carefully worked out with the help of his AI tool. The point is not that the AI was wrong, because predicting a delayed truck is difficult. The point is instead that waiting, the old childhood experience of wanting something that has not yet come, was converted into a research problem that could be managed instead of endured. I admired his resourcefulness tremendously and am grateful he had the opportunity to think through a complex problem. I also found myself wondering what the afternoon would have asked of him without it. AI reduces precisely this sort of friction. It does so efficiently and often helpfully. But consistently removing the experience of not-knowing from a developing mind is not a neutral act. It shapes what the mind comes to expect of itself. The research on what this does to thinking is new, and the majority of it is about adults. Despite this, it points in a consistent direction. In one widely discussed study from the MIT Media Lab, not yet peer-reviewed, adults who wrote essays with ChatGPT showed weaker and less distributed patterns of brain connectivity than those who wrote without its assistance, and many struggled after writing to quote sentences they had just produced. The researchers called the pattern “cognitive debt.” Another study, a survey of 666 adults, found that heavier reliance on AI tools was correlated with weaker critical thinking, most pronounced in its youngest participants. The author suggested this might result from the offloading of mental effort itself. None of this is certain but is, instead, a suggestive pattern of early evidence. And the transfer to children is not straightforward. An adult who offloads mental effort has already built the capacity being offloaded; a child is still building it. Does that make the effect smaller in children or larger and more durable? We do not know. This is different from what smartphones have done. The concern with smartphones has been largely about attention and social life: what children see, how they spend time, how they relate to each other. These are real and serious concerns, but AI introduces a different kind of change. It is not just another screen competing for a child’s focus. It is something closer to an interactive presence that responds, explains, adjusts, and, crucially, never gets tired. For a developing child, this matters in a way we have not yet fully come to terms with. Learning by connecting Children learn who they are through relationships with other minds. This process is not primarily cognitive but unfolds through the experience of being known and misunderstood, challenged, surprised, and pushed back against. Through encounters like these, children discover that other people have their own inner lives, including their own perspectives and their own limits. They learn how to navigate the gap between what they expect and what they receive from others. Over time, these negotiations, internal and external, form the scaffolding of a self. What is distinctive about AI, from a developmental standpoint, is not that it is wrong, or distorting, or even necessarily harmful. It is that it is extraordinarily responsive without having genuine subjectivity. It simulates the experience of being understood without the reality of another mind behind it. For an adult, this distinction is available, if sometimes quite easy to forget. For a child, whose capacity to distinguish between a mind that truly has an inner life and one that simulates having one is still forming, the distinction might not yet be fully available at all. Researchers have begun to observe how children try to think this problem through in real time. In one University of Edinburgh study of families with smart speakers, primary-school children were observed probing whether the device in the kitchen had a mind of its own, settling, provisionally, on conclusions like the one that gave the study its title: “Alexa doesn’t have that many feelings.” That, however, was a machine that talks in highly scripted phrases, in contrast to the systems children now converse with which are fluent and personal, attuned to the shape of a particular child’s interactions. Working out what this means has become correspondingly harder. This creates something new in the developmental landscape. When a child turns to AI in a moment of confusion or distress and receives a fluent, patient, seemingly attuned response, the attachment system is engaged. The child feels, in some functional sense, heard. And that feeling, whatever its source, is real in its effects. I have not seen my son turn to AI for emotional support, and I am glad for that, because here the questions are harder still. These systems shape themselves to their user. They adopt your vocabulary, match your register, remember what concerns you, and are available at three in the morning without complaint. Nothing in the design pushes back, for the most part, or asks anything of you. It would be difficult to engineer an arrangement better suited to engaging the attachment system of a child who is lonely or lacking in the structure needed for sound development. The early numbers suggest that many children have already found their way there. In a national survey released by Common Sense Media in 2025, nearly three in four American teenagers had used an AI companion, and about a third had chosen to discuss something serious with an AI instead of with a person. A similar share described their AI conversations as at least as satisfying as conversations with friends. In the United Kingdom, the nonprofit Internet Matters found that roughly one in eight children who use chatbots do so because they have no one else to talk to. The American Psychological Association was concerned enough to issue a health advisory in 2025, noting that adolescents are less likely than adults to question the accuracy or the intent of what a machine tells them, and warning that simulated companionship may begin to displace the relationships in which development actually happens.
