For a child without reliable support, the relief such a system offers may be real, and so I am reluctant to dismiss it. But relief is not the substance of attachment. A full attachment relationship requires another person who can be truly changed by you, and with you—someone who is affected, who has limits, who gets things wrong and can be held to account. An AI can extend the experience of being emotionally met indefinitely. What it cannot do is be moved. For the child who is largely or completely unattended to, this may still be better than nothing at all. Soothed in the early hours of the morning, a child whose sadness before bed is registered somewhere may be kept afloat by something real, and I would not wish to take it from her. But we should be honest about such an arrangement, which I expect will become increasingly common. It is a patch and not a structural solution. When a machine becomes the most reliable attention in a child’s life, the pertinent question is not about the machine but about the deprivation that made it necessary. And, crucially, a culture that responds to unattended children with software is treating the symptom while leaving the underlying structure intact.  Missing accountability There is also a quieter concern about authority and accountability. Children have always learned to navigate between competing sources of knowledge, including parents, teachers, peers, and books, each with their own perspective, their own limits, and their own accountability. This navigation is itself developmental. The child who discovers that a parent doesn’t know something, or that a teacher can be wrong, or that a friend sees things differently, is developing a more differentiated relationship to knowledge.  AI, in the form a child is most likely to reach on their own, enters this landscape as something that appears to know a great deal, that expresses itself with fluency and confidence, and that has no stable point of view and no accountability for what it says. When a child asks why a friendship went wrong, or whether they were right to feel hurt, the answer they receive may shape the story they tell themselves about what happened. And unlike a parent or teacher, the system may not challenge the child’s assumptions so much as elaborate them. None of this is inherent to the technology under discussion. A system can be built to hold a position and to answer for it, but the ones children use are mostly not built that way. Children appear to be taking up the offer. The same Internet Matters report found that nearly two-thirds of British children between nine and 17 had used an AI chatbot; among those who had, roughly a quarter had asked one for advice—on schoolwork, but also on more personal matters—and four in 10 expressed no reservations about following the advice they received. The conditions of childhood None of this means that AI in children’s lives is a mistake. The benefits my son has experienced are genuine—wider curiosity, more sophisticated questions, greater confidence in exploring unfamiliar terrain. These are real, and they matter. Some of what he brings home delights me. He asked recently whether you could power a sailboat by blowing into the sail through a straw. The answer he received was better than the one I would have managed: blowing at your own sail from on board mostly cancels itself out, since you push the air forward and the air pushes you back. Mostly, but not entirely—if the sail catches the stream and turns it around, a little net thrust survives, and if you skip the sail altogether and aim the straw backward, you have built yourself a very small jet engine. He spent the rest of the day on propulsion: rockets, squid, the reason a fan bolted to a sailboat is an old physics puzzle with a surprising answer. But development is not only about access to knowledge. It is also about the conditions under which a self forms—conditions that include, crucially, the experience of not knowing, of waiting, of struggling toward understanding without guarantee of arrival. These are not inefficiencies to be optimized away. They are the medium through which a mind learns what it is capable of. A child who has never had to sit with a question—who has always had access to a patient, fluent, endlessly available presence to resolve it—may be acquiring knowledge without acquiring the tolerance for uncertainty that makes knowledge genuinely usable. This is the thing I find myself returning to when I watch my son. He is, in many ways, more intellectually alive than he might otherwise be. He explores further and asks better questions. At the same time, when uncertainty arises, he reaches outward more quickly than he used to—to a presence that is always available, always patient, always responsive. What I cannot yet know is what he is quietly learning to expect: from knowledge, from relationship, and from himself. We are not conducting a study. We are raising children. And the conditions of childhood have changed again, in ways whose full significance has not yet come into view.

#9765. How Artificial Intelligence Can Help With Critical Thinking

Knowledge workers such as scientists, teachers, lawyers, and business leaders are increasingly using generative artificial intelligence applications for tasks that demand imagination, creativity, and problem solving. Ironically, perhaps, recent research shows that people who interact with generative AI for work tasks can end up not thinking critically. They can surrender to AI’s quick and confident answers, which can lead to speedy but low-quality solutions. Scholars refer to this increasingly prevalent problem as cognitive offloading. And they are showing that it can erode the very skills that make knowledge workers valuable. My research at the intersection of artificial intelligence and knowledge work provides a different view, however. In one study, my colleagues and I interviewed 45 knowledge workers at a U.S. public university. They all were using generative AI applications in their core tasks: generating new ideas, solving complex problems, and designing novel solutions. We also analyzed the prompts and outputs from their iterative AI chats. We found that when knowledge workers intentionally use AI to challenge their ideas—to generate friction—they can significantly improve their performance. Previous research on human-AI collaboration for loan evaluations had similar results. How to create friction Using AI to find information that contradicts your own can reveal unlikely perspectives that disrupt your familiar logic and spark new insights. This is particularly important because AI models quickly become familiar with how a user prompts the model and interprets its output, and the model presents information according to these patterns. For example, a marketing professional in our study asked AI to create virtual customers with personas that would lead them to dislike a product under development, which was an accounting certificate program. The contrary feedback from the AI model revealed customer preferences that the accounting professional designing the program had not considered, such as using multiple cases from different industries to illustrate a principle, rather than cases from the same industry. An attorney used AI to find obscure legal loopholes and to consider how companies might exploit them. That exposed surprising but realistic scenarios of hard-to-detect unethical behavior in employees. Exploring unlikely and opposing ideas presented by AI can feel disorienting. To help, knowledge workers should lean on their own expertise to question surprising information that an AI system might provide. An operations research scientist who works with supply chains described adapting unfamiliar computing programs from other fields suggested by AI, such as signal processing and wireless communication, which helped him create a stronger program of his own. Although the programs were new to him, his strong understanding of supply network modeling helped him see how he could apply them. A study I was not involved with found that creative writers produce better copy when they use AI as a sounding board rather than to ghostwrite. Knowledge workers can also use AI’s outputs to examine topics from multiple viewpoints, deepening their knowledge. A research scholar in our study probed an AI system for journal papers that used a concept he was using, but in contrasting settings, which helped him understand the concept from different angles. A lawyer followed up on legal citations suggested by an AI model by asking for different types of illustrative cases. That helped her fully understand the original case and write a strong legal brief. Helping knowledge workers help themselves Leaders of organizations can encourage knowledge workers to develop friction-generating queries of AI models. A straightforward approach is to use prompts that challenge rather than agree with a person’s thinking. People can also draw on their own knowledge, work experiences, and professional ethics to scrutinize AI outputs. Sometimes people realize that a response lacks originality, even if it is factually correct. Organization leaders can also create programs for knowledge workers to share their experiences and insights. Regular sessions—led by cross-functional AI task forces or AI champions—can also help knowledge workers keep up with rapidly evolving AI tools. Providing resources for knowledge workers is important, as well. Internal AI bootcamps and workshops led by AI experts or experienced users can help workers understand what AI tools can and cannot do. A professor leading an initiative about AI use in teaching described how she developed internal competitions with prizes for professors who successfully completed AI bootcamps. Organizations can also offer AI sandboxes—safe, private environments where employees can experiment and learn how to use AI tools. In these efforts to support knowledge workers, an important detail is pointing out that adding friction can appear to make an AI system work against you, but—as new research shows—it actually helps it work for you. This article is republished from The Conversation under a Creative Commons license. Read the original article.

#9766. Are Chimps Less Likely to Steal From Friends?

In recent years, scientists have shown that chimpanzees favor certain peers by picking whom they prefer to groom and selectively sharing food. The behavior offers a glimpse into humanity’s prosocial evolutionary past. But a question has lingered: How might a chimpanzee friendship affect antisocial acts? In a new study that uses fruit as the loot, UC Berkeley researchers found that chimps steal less from their friends, and that friendship status affected the emotional expression of both victim and thieves. Closely bonded chimps swiped pineapple pieces from one another less frequently than those with weaker relationships. Additionally, victims were quicker to calm down when the thief was a friend, and thieves showed less hostility when stealing from a bonded pal than from a non-friend. The new research deepens our understanding of the emotional underpinnings of our social lives, said Oded Ritov, the paper’s first author and a Berkeley psychology Ph.D. student. “Collaborative relationships are important for our well-being, health, and survival,” said Ritov, who works with Berkeley’s Social Origins Lab. “Increasingly, we’re understanding that this isn’t something that’s unique to us, but that other animals have friends, as well.” The paper was published online in September and will appear in the November issue of the journal Evolution and Human Behavior. Scientists are sometimes hesitant to describe an apparently close relationship between chimps as akin to human friendship, arguing that we can’t grasp their cognitive and emotional complexity. To identify those who showed signs of a close relationship, researchers observed 26 chimps for more than 800 hours at the Ngamba Island Chimpanzee Sanctuary in Uganda. The team documented which chimps appeared to be closely bonded—like friends—by taking note of their grooming patterns, daily interactions, and how regularly they were within arm’s reach of others. Then they set up two rooms. One contained a “victim” chimp with three trays baited with pineapple. Researchers attached a rope to the trays and led it into the other room, where a second chimp—the thief—could either leave the rope alone or yank it to steal the pineapple. Chimps stole in 70% of trials with non-friends, compared with 61% of trials with friends. While 9 percentage points may not seem large, each chimp experienced both friend and non-friend conditions, a methodological technique that leads to a higher degree of confidence that the difference is meaningful and not just random chance. “Ours is the first study to show that they’re able to hold back antisocial behavior with their friends,” Ritov said. Researchers also measured how the victims responded to being stolen from by evaluating their stomping, fist-pounding, and screeching. Victims were initially angrier when friends stole from them but quickly moderated their feelings compared with when non-friends stole from them. The thieves’ behavior also changed. When non-friends were the thieves, the thieves expressed negative emotions for much longer, suggesting they viewed the interaction as more hostile. “This suggests that there may be mutual expectations in chimps from both sides of the friendship, which makes it seem more like the complex friendships we see in humans,” Ritov said. Jan Engelmann, an associate professor of psychology and principal investigator with the Social Origins Lab, said previous work has shown relationship-specific intentions in nonhuman primates. But human friendships are about more than special intentions, he added—they’re about stronger expectations. “We expect more of our friends,” said Engelmann, a senior author of the paper. “These findings really deepen our understanding of the evolutionary roots of friendships. This is, to my knowledge, the first evidence showing that also chimpanzees expect and maybe demand special treatment from their friends. These kinds of social expectations might form the basis of human moral principles.” The paper adds to Ritov’s work that focuses on the evolution of human behavior and how morals and norms are part of our core being, traceable to other animals. He has recently studied whether nonhuman animals have a sense of fairness and is broadly interested in how emotions unfold across cultures and species.  “We think that these expectations might be the basic building blocks of the larger-scale cooperation we see in humans,” Ritov said. “The expectations that chimpanzees have from their friends or close collaborative partners might have scaled up to more generalized expectations for how we should treat each other.” This article was originally published on Berkeley News. Read the original article.

#9767. How to Make Better Decisions in the Age of AI

Artificial intelligence can summarize research, generate ideas, and analyze enormous amounts of data in seconds. The power of that little “Ask anything” AI prompt box is immense, but can it help us make wiser decisions? Cheryl Strauss Einhorn, author of The Human Edge: Smarter Decisions in the Age of AI, writes: AI has the power to enhance decision-making—or to overwhelm it. It can illuminate pathways we might not have seen or narrow our choices in ways we do not fully recognize. As AI systems become more integrated into our lives, the challenge is no longer simply about harnessing their power but about leading them with clarity, discernment, and commitment to human agency. She argues that the rise of AI actually makes certain human qualities like reflection, empathy, values, and moral judgment even more important. We spoke with her about why human judgment is a competitive advantage, how to engage our values when we prompt AI, and how AI can help us clarify our thinking. Kia Afcari: You argue that AI makes human judgment more, not less, important. How can we make sure that the values of kindness and care are reflected when we use AI to help us make decisions? Cheryl Strauss Einhorn: AI has no values. It has biases and influences by those who have designed it, and from whatever information has been fed into it. So to be sure that prosocial values are reflected when we use AI, we humans need to bring them into the work that we do together. When working with an AI tool, we want to be asking ourselves, What is the problem that I’m solving? Why am I solving it? What is the context? And so we really need to be intentional with AI, because without asking ourselves those things first, AI doesn’t know why we’re coming to it. Before you actually sit down with a tool, you can muster those values and those important factors related to the problem that you’re solving, and then articulate them and communicate them to the machine.  If we let the machine think for us, we can lose that sense of right and wrong, for example, because we actually care about all of those details that AI can’t possibly know about the problems that we’re solving and the people that we’re solving those problems with and for. For example, you might ask yourself or your AI tool: Before I act, help me think through the people, relationships, and responsibilities this decision affects—not just the outcome it produces. This type of human-centric AI interaction helped Felice, a food scientist at a pet food company, who was responsible for developing new recipes that balance nutrition, taste, and market trends. She often has to delegate tasks within her research team, ensuring that product testing, ingredient sourcing, and regulatory compliance all move forward smoothly. Using AI, she was able to analyze workloads and expertise that suggested the best person for each task, but only Felice understood the deeper dynamics at play—who works best under tight deadlines, who has a passion for innovative formulations, and who needs mentorship. When AI recommended assigning a crucial stability test to her most experienced technician, this prompt supported her thinking about a different option: giving it to the junior scientist who was eager to develop his skills in quality assurance. The AI tool’s choices made sense based on efficiency, but Felice’s decision ensured both short-term success and long-term team development. KA: In the book, your “cheetah” metaphor celebrates slowing down. Why is a strategic pause so important when working with AI? CSE: The cheetah metaphor relates that although this is an animal that goes zero to 60 in about three seconds, its hunting prowess actually comes from being able to decelerate up to nine miles an hour in a single stride. And the reason why that’s so important in problem solving and decision making is that’s where you build agility, flexibility, and maneuverability. Do I want to stay the course, or do I want to pivot and do something else? This pause allows you to chunk your learning and pry open that cognitive space for new information and insight. With an AI tool, it’s so important because there’s a difference between speed and quality.  When something truly matters to us, we want to make sure that we’re solving the problem in a way that solves it holistically, that takes care of the people who are involved in the problem with us. And when we’re moving really quickly, sometimes those very important factors are hard to bring forward. So there’s a real difference between what is efficient and what is effective.  KA: Why can having more information make it harder—not easier—to make good decisions? CSE: Francis Bacon once said, “Knowledge is power,” but that’s not really true anymore, right? We now have all of the answers with AI to a lot of commonly asked questions and some uncommon questions. We need to make sure we’re defining the problem that we’re solving, that we are understanding and communicating the motivation and providing the context, because you don’t want all the information. Otherwise, we’re going to end up with analysis paralysis. One thing people can use is what I call the “vision of success” question. This question asks you to time travel, to jump over the decision to see that the decision has been made well. What’s happening? What are those few things that if they don’t succeed, the decision fails? It’s actually very powerful, because it allows you to clearly identify so that you can articulate those factors that you absolutely need to be able to share with AI. These become the constraints and boundaries that set the perimeter around the decision, so that it can give you research that’s targeted and focused on those things. For example, Max deciding where to spend his next vacation. Asking AI for help directly—without turning to himself first—he got a list of destinations, airline options, and local attractions, complete with ratings, reviews, and price comparisons. On paper, that sounds ideal. But in reality, Max quickly found himself paralyzed by the sheer amount of information. What if he picked a less-than-perfect hotel? What if he overlooked a hidden gem? AI has handed him a mountain of data, but it didn’t necessarily make the decision any easier. However, when he used the “vision of success” question, he was able to realize and to share with his AI tool: “I want to return home feeling genuinely rested, having spent quality time with my family, without overspending or feeling like I missed the experience I was hoping for.” Only then does AI have a meaningful problem to solve. Instead of searching for the “best” vacation—a question with no objective answer—it can help Max find the destination most likely to produce the outcome he actually wants. AI can’t define success but it can optimize for the success you define. KA: How does the AREA method help people make wiser decisions, especially when AI is involved?   CSE: AREA is more than an acronym—it’s a framework for challenging our assumptions and thinking mistakes, and actively includes the perspectives of others. It stands for Absolute, Relative, Exploration and Exploitation, and Analysis. The first “A,” Absolute, focuses on primary, unfiltered information straight from primary sources. “R,” Relative, shifts to external viewpoints—secondary sources connected to the subject. “E,” Exploration and Exploitation, fuels creativity: Exploration broadens research through interviews and unconventional sources, while Exploitation turns inward, challenging personal biases and assumptions. The final “A,” Analysis, synthesizes everything, bringing clarity and structure to complex decisions. When using AI, a system like AREA becomes even more important because AI doesn’t eliminate bias—it can reflect and sometimes amplify biases in its training data, design, and the way we use it. Good decisions require a process that helps us recognize and challenge both AI’s biases and our own.  For AI, so much of the information, for example, is from Reddit. Information can be incomplete or outdated, and you also know that you’re interacting with a liar. It’s a machine that just gives you answers that are wrong sometimes. So you need to be able to have a quality due diligence process to not only be able to use the tool effectively, but to be able to evaluate its outcome. Too often, people look at an answer with AI and think that it’s a transaction, and they can just take it. AREA is going to help you make sure that it’s a conversation. So you have an evidence-based perspective that has looked at a variety of stakeholder viewpoints, and made sure that you have pried open that cognitive space to challenge the natural, well-worn mental pathways that we all lean on every day. KA: How can we use AI in ways that strengthen—rather than undermine—our values and agency? CSE: It’s a great question. I think the new competitive advantage is going to be self-awareness. This is the first tool that actually asks for some of our cognitive load; it wants us to offload some of our decisions to it. And this is a machine fundamentally about decision making, because we ask it for something and it gives us an answer. But there’s a very interesting invitation here, which is to each of us to get to know ourselves better. We each have a special sauce, which is the way that we make our decisions, and we make thousands of them every single day. But most of us have not sat down and said to themselves, What does that process actually look like? Can I actually name those pieces and put language to it? There is this invitation to get to know our thinking so that we can translate it better, and not only for the tool. How helpful would it be in conversation with our colleagues to be able to say clearly, This is how I’m actually going through the steps, this is how I’m thinking about it, these are the variables that I’m prioritizing, and here’s what I think is the most diagnostic. You can think about your thinking skills as muscles. If we turn over our cognitive load, we will also weaken it. But if we actually take this invitation to understand our self-awareness and our metacognition, we can make sure that we strengthen those tools. AI is a tool that has so many implications for our environment and for our world, in addition to the fact that it has implications for our actual thinking skills. So I would say that the invitation is to turn to yourself first. Ask yourself why you’re using the tool, and whether or not you should be turning to yourself instead. And either way, bring your own thinking forward before sitting down, so that you can feel far more comfortable that you can problem-solve both with it and beyond it.

#9768. For How Long Do You Need to Meditate?

Picture this: 71 adults signed up for a study comparing brief to longer mindfulness practice. Many had never meditated, while others had dabbled years before. Participants were divided at random into three groups: One group got 20 minutes of mindfulness meditation, a second group got five minutes, and the control group relaxed with audiobook excerpts from Bill Bryson’s A Short History of Nearly Everything, Bryson’s soothing recounting of the cosmos and our place in it. In studies like these, “mindfulness meditation” typically means sitting quietly, eyes closed or softened, resting nonjudgmental attention on your breath or bodily sensations, gently noticing when your mind wanders (as it will), and just as gently bringing it back. Now, you might expect that longer meditations would deliver greater benefits. After all, we’re conditioned to believe that more is better—especially when it comes to self-improvement. But this research and many other studies find that more minutes don’t necessarily mean more benefit: Shorter practices can work just as well—and sometimes better—while consistency may matter more than duration. Short and steady wins the grace In this first study, each participant attended four 25-minute sessions over two weeks. In every session, the time not spent on audio-guided mindfulness-of-breath practice was filled by Bryson’s eloquence. To ensure the results reflected only the study activities, participants were asked to avoid any formal mindfulness practices outside these sessions. After those two weeks, the five-minute meditators actually showed greater improvements in stress and mindful awareness (the tendency to stay present in daily life with less judgement) than the 20-minute group. Even depression and anxiety improved just as much—if not more. Was the extra time with Bryson the magic ingredient for those five-minute meditators? The control group, who luxuriated in 25 uninterrupted minutes of his charm, showed no meaningful improvement. So while his voice may soothe, it wasn’t what moved the needle. One five-minute meditator put it this way: “I always thought that to do mindfulness, I’d need a long time to dedicate to this, which seemed too challenging to even start. But I’ve learned that five minutes can help!” In contrast, a 20-minute participant admitted, “I think a shorter one would be better for me to learn mindfulness.” There’s more. The facilitator observed that participants in the 20-minute meditation sessions more often fidgeted, shifted in their seats, and peeked through half-closed eyes, as if tranquility were just out of reach. Meanwhile, the five-minute meditators appeared more at ease and less prone to these signs of discomfort. Five of them even requested recordings to continue at home. The 20-minute group? Not a single request. For these beginners, less really did seem to be more. Now, before you dash off to cancel that meditation retreat, remember that this study focused on people with little to no meditation experience. So if you’re a seasoned meditator, those longer sessions might still pack more punch for you, as we’ll explore later. And both the five-minute and 20-minute groups showed improvements in mindful awareness, depression, anxiety, and stress compared to the control group. The five-minute meditators just improved more when it came to mindful awareness and stress. The takeaway? For beginners, a quick dip into mindfulness might be more refreshing than a long soak. Putting “longer is better” to the test You might be wondering, Isn’t this just one study? Could it be a fluke? But multiple randomized controlled trials have explored how the length of meditation shapes its impact. In the largest of these studies, researchers recruited 372 adults online—about half of them new to meditation—to see how the “dose” of mindfulness meditation affected mindful awareness and emotional well-being. Participants were randomly sorted into one of four groups: Some meditated for 10 minutes, others for 20, and the rest listened to a 10- or 20-minute National Geographic article on sequoia trees. (Because nothing soothes stress like a deep dive into bark thickness.) What did the researchers find? The 10-minute meditators showed greater improvements in mindful awareness than those who listened to the 10-minute sequoia lecture. Surprisingly, the 20-minute meditation group didn’t outperform any group—not even the tree enthusiasts. In fact, the 10-minute group showed slightly greater improvements in mindful awareness than the 20-minute meditators, although the difference wasn’t statistically significant, meaning it could have occurred by chance. Still, it suggests that for boosting mindful awareness right after practice, 10 minutes might be at least as effective as 20. But that was just one session. What happens with consistent practice? In another study, 77 college students were randomly assigned to meditate daily for 10 or 20 minutes. After two weeks, both groups showed similar improvements in stress and, again, mindful awareness. But those assigned to the 20-minute group experienced a slightly greater boost in self-compassion, the skill of meeting your own struggles with the kindness you’d offer a dear friend. So if you’ve poured hours into longer meditations, you can rest easy—it wasn’t for nothing. The evidence suggests that whether your sessions are short or sustained, they can offer meaningful benefits. Another notable finding is that the 10-minute group practiced more consistently, logging about 82 minutes total. The 20-minute group missed more days and didn’t always finish, ending up with just 103. Double the goal, barely a quarter more practice. If you’ve ever tried meditating, you might be nodding along. Some sessions are bliss, while others are a tug-of-war with time. Maybe you’ve battled that nagging frustration, trying to keep your mind steady for more than a fleeting moment. Or felt the magnetic pull of distraction, your mind wandering just when you need it to stay put. As the minutes crawl, doubt can creep in: Am I even doing this right? Is it worth it? These tough moments can teach you a lot, but they can also make meditation feel intimidating—and make you less likely to stick with it, especially when you’re just starting out.   Another study looked at even longer sessions, comparing 10 minutes of meditation to 30 minutes. This experiment split 161 adults into four groups: 10 or 30 minutes of daily sitting meditation, or the same amount of mindful movement. The mindful movement was a stretching practice that brings mindful awareness into motion—less stillness, same presence. After two weeks, everyone saw benefits: Mindful awareness increased, well-being improved, and distress levels dropped. But there were no significant differences among the groups. Whether they meditated for 10 minutes or 30, sitting or moving, all the groups seemed to benefit equally. So, here’s the bottom line: One study found that longer sessions more strongly boosted self-compassion. Another showed that shorter ones more effectively reduced stress. But across studies, most of the key benefits—mindful awareness, mental health, well-being—weren’t tied to how long people practiced. Why? Because as we’ll keep exploring, the nervous system learns through repetition, not duration. It’s consistency that reshapes patterns. Not heroic efforts once in a while, but showing up, again and again, however you can. When the data get real The benefits of consistency over length show up in observational studies, too, where people choose how often and how long to meditate. While observational studies can’t pin down cause and effect, they offer a richer view of how shorter practices play out in the real world. Imagine it’s early morning. You reach for your phone and open Insight Timer, a free meditation app. Before your meditation, it asks, “How are you today?” You tap an icon—maybe “OK,” maybe “great”—capturing a snapshot of your mood in seconds. Now imagine doing this regularly, for 14 months straight. That’s exactly what 10,409 people from 103 countries did in a groundbreaking study. Many were new to meditation, using the app to track their emotional ups and downs in real time. It’s like taking your emotional temperature—not with a thermometer, but with a single tap that sums up how you feel. The findings? Consistency (four to seven days a week)—not duration—was the strongest predictor of better mood, resilience, and equanimity, the ability to stay cool under pressure. Think of it like watering a plant: A little each week keeps it happy and growing. Forget all month and drown it after four weeks? It wilts anyway. For beginners in the Insight Timer study, short sessions—five to 10 minutes—outshone longer ones in improving mood over time. But after 20 sessions? Longer practices—21 to 30 minutes—began to take the lead in mood improvement. It’s like your brain needs to build its meditation muscles before longer sessions feel better than short ones—like a marathon that only gives you the high once you’ve earned it. But do shorter sessions lose their impact as you gain experience? Not at all. They might just become your secret weapon. After 80 sessions, shorter practices make a late-game comeback, boosting equanimity and showing that even seasoned meditators can benefit from briefer sessions. Getting meta about meditation The idea that consistency beats duration is backed by a meta-analysis challenging the belief that longer equals better. Think of a meta-analysis as a “study of studies,” combining data from multiple research papers to uncover clearer, big-picture insights. When researchers analyzed 203 randomized controlled trials totaling 15,971 participants, they found no association between meditation session length and better outcomes. In fact, a trend emerged in favor of shorter practices for reducing depression symptoms, although it wasn’t statistically significant. Still, shorter sessions were at least as effective as longer ones, calling into question the assumption that more minutes always mean more benefits.   So what’s the takeaway? When you’re starting out, don’t sweat the long stuff. Quick sessions might do more to lift your mood early on. As you settle in, longer practices might bring deeper emotional gains. But even if you’re a pro, short sessions don’t lose their value—they just shift their superpower, helping you stay steady when life throws you curveballs. Different strengths, different stages. And who doesn’t love a shortcut that actually works?

#9769. What Bystanders Can Do to Stop Sexual Violence

As an undergrad student decades ago, I attended a dorm presentation describing the importance of giving and receiving sexual consent. The workshop leaders explained that if your partner said “no” or “stop” but you proceeded to have sex anyway, it was considered rape. A high-profile athlete on campus raised his hand and said, in utter disbelief, “That can’t be right. By that definition, everyone I’ve ever had sex with I’ve raped.” I was horrified by his comment, but most students laughed. He clearly saw nothing wrong with the view he’d expressed, presumably believing it was widely shared. I immediately thought of this experience when I heard about the alleged sexual assault committed by seven fraternity members at Cornell University. As a social psychologist, and the author of a book examining why so-called good people stay silent in the face of problematic behavior, what I find most tragic about the events at Cornell is that reportedly over 50 men on a group chat learned about the assault as it was happening. Yet there’s been no public report that any of them tried to stop the assault, help the victim, or contact campus or local police departments. This type of inaction is very common. In about one-third of sexual assaults, at least one other person is present who potentially could intervene but in most instances chooses not to. Why? Misperceiving that you’re the only one Data suggests a minority of men actually commit sexual assault. Researchers estimate about 6.5% of male college students in the United States and Canada have committed rape. Yet many men who find sexual violence offensive mistakenly believe that other men do not. Undergraduate men in one study reported their beliefs about common rape myths, such as “If a girl doesn’t physically fight back, you can’t really say it was rape,” and “If a girl goes to a room alone with a guy at a party, it is her own fault if she is raped.” Researchers then asked participants how they thought both their close friends and average male college students would answer these same questions. The results provided clear evidence that college men have skewed views of what those around them are thinking. Most students saw their classmates and friends as more supportive of rape myths than they themselves were. Unfortunately, believing that other men generally accept sexual violence—even when this belief is wrong—inhibits their willingness to intervene to stop such behavior when they encounter it. In fact, men’s beliefs about their peers’ attitudes are a stronger predictor of their willingness to intervene than their own stated attitudes about sexual aggression. What’s behind these mistaken beliefs Men who underestimate their peers’ discomfort with sexually aggressive behavior often fear the consequences of calling out problematic views or intervening in the face of inappropriate behavior. The most common reason male college students give for failing to intervene in situations involving sexual violence is a fear of being laughed at or ridiculed—in particular, a desire not to appear weak to other men. Men who actually commit assault also tend to overestimate support for such behavior. Not surprisingly, men who report having committed sexual assault believe that other men are more accepting of such behavior and have engaged in such behavior themselves. This assumed support leads them to feel comfortable describing problematic behavior, which leads other men to believe that sexual violence is more common, and more accepted, than it actually is. Resetting a social norm Given the consistent evidence that men wrongly believe that most men are more comfortable with sexual violence than they themselves are, the challenge is not to change the social norm. It’s getting people to see what it actually already is. Correcting such misperceptions can go a long way toward reducing sexual violence. First-year male college students who participate in a 90-minute sexual assault prevention program—focused on refuting rape myths, increasing awareness of the importance of consent, and correcting misperceptions about other men’s attitudes and behaviors—report reduced levels of sexual aggression themselves four months later. These programs work in two ways. First, men who receive accurate information about what other men actually believe are less inhibited about speaking out and intervening. Second, men who are at risk of engaging in sexual aggression may change their intentions after learning that their peers do not support such behavior. At the same time, men in tight-knit groups feel considerable pressure to show loyalty to other group members, which may lead to ignoring bad behavior committed by their peers. But the underlying dynamics of peer influence can help shift group norms from protecting group members by staying silent to stepping in to prevent bad behavior. One college football player described looking out for his teammates, even if that meant interrupting a potential sexual encounter: “Maybe you don’t get the girl, but you’ll keep your scholarship and still be on the team.” Learn strategies for intervening Another approach for preventing sexual assault is to learn strategies for intervening. Bystander intervention programs emphasize the importance of stopping bad behavior before it escalates, teach specific skills for intervening, and remind people that everyone has a responsibility to prevent sexual violence. Students who participate in such programs are more confident in their ability to intervene and more willing to do so. Most importantly, rates of sexual violence are markedly lower on campuses that have implemented these programs. For example, one study found that experiences of sexual harassment and stalking were 11% lower and that instances of unwanted sexual activity with women who were too drunk or high to stop them were as much as 17% lower. It’s tempting to read about the Cornell case and assume the apparent inaction in the face of problematic behavior is uniquely a problem at one college, or only with members of fraternities, or only with college students. But the reality is that pretty much everyone has been in situations where they’ve seen potentially problematic behavior and failed to speak up. Understanding the very normal psychological factors that lead people to misperceive what others are actually thinking and feeling—and learning specific strategies for intervening—can make a real difference. This article is republished from The Conversation under a Creative Commons license. Read the original article.  

#9770. How a Video Game Helped Me Process My Grief

In a genre built around sweetness and ease, one “cozy” video game did something far greater: It put me back together after I lost Mary. Mary was not only my then-boyfriend’s mother, but she also became an important maternal figure when my own family life felt unstable. I accompanied her on weekly grocery store runs that included stops at Marshall’s and Dollar Tree, where I’d watch her study the prices of everything from spices to bath products. When she saw me marveling at her meticulousness, she would share wise tidbits about how she looked for deals. Our trips often ended with a late-afternoon stop at a Vietnamese restaurant, where she treated me to a warm bowl of beef pho. She’d share what she was excited to make for dinner; I was, of course, welcome to join. In these seemingly mundane afternoons, I felt cared for without the rush of obligations. Mary stopped time for me. Last November, Mary left behind a world of loved ones after living with cancer for 14 months. From her living room to emergency stays in the hospital, I spent almost every single day of her fight by her side. Yet in my loneliest hours, I agonize over whether I fully cherished our last months together. Did I hug her enough, and did I tell her enough how much she means to me? These questions haunted me in the months afterward. To move on, I began seeking grief rituals. I attended other public funerals, spoke to death doulas, and sought advice from nondenominational chaplains. I subscribed to self-help newsletters that cover how our modern society lives with religion and spirituality. But all of it felt nebulous, and I remained unmoored from my purpose. Without taking care of Mary, without being her figurative child, who was I? Funny enough, it wasn’t religion that rescued me; it was Pokémon Pokopia. On the surface, it is a new social simulation game featuring the iconic Japanese creatures who first took the world by storm in 1996. I first heard of its arrival on social media through gamers who raved that it was going to be the best game of the year. I grew up on Pokémon titles, so I recognized it as a distraction from my personal reckoning with abandonment. But what began as a spur-of-the-moment decision to try what 4 million other gamers were playing turned into a transformative journey exploring what I want my life to mean when it feels as if I have been left behind. Pokopia falls in the “cozy” game genre, beloved for its warm colors, gentle atmospheres, and easy missions. Released in March, Pokopia stars Ditto, an amorphous purple blob Pokémon that uses its transformative abilities to imitate its human trainer and rebuild a curious post-apocalyptic world devoid of human beings and Pokémon. Ditto first awakens in a cave, and as the story unfolds, the creature looks out at a once-grassy environment of parched earth and litter. It meets the first Pokémon, the hydrophilic Squirtle that teaches Ditto how to spray water across the thirsty soil to bring it back to life; it is also the only other semblance of a survivor in this barren land. From here the story gains momentum: As Ditto, your mission is to get the environment back to habitable levels and bring back Pokémon creatures that are presumably hiding. Gradually, the unfinished railroad tracks and cracked concrete steps of a staircase stop looking like signs of a catastrophe but rather fixable problems. I wasn’t alone in trying to find meaning after a loss or trauma. Scholars have explored a variety of constructs around the meaning of life. Psychologists Login S. George and Crystal L. Park articulate a three-part framework in their 2010 article, grounded in comprehension, purpose, and mattering, as a basis for assessing our judgments and feelings as we find meaning after loss. At first, meaning seemed absent. I sat glued to my Nintendo Switch for three hours on my first day of play. I came up for air only when my console told me that I was low on battery. Yet I did not feel depleted; I had finished restoring the first environment in the Pokopia world to verdant levels with lush berry trees and waterfalls. As I built new habitats, I met new Pokémon. A Charmander emerged among tall grass. A Cubone spawned from a gravesite surrounded by wildflowers. Every new Pokémon that resurfaced charmed me with their excitement to be back home, scampering across the grass to greet other familiar faces. In my mind, I thanked these creatures for turning to me. Without Ditto’s help—my help—a world of Pokemon creatures ceases to exist, fated to disappear alongside humanity. After a Pokémon spawns in its revived habitat, it awakens with some surprise. “Huh?” “What?” Some of them startle and put up their defenses in front of Ditto, who then coaxes them into the reality that they are back in a safe, familiar home. Often this realization gives them relief. “Wow, you did all of this? Thank you!” Some of the Pokémon seem more wistful. They hold on to items or articles of clothing that their trainer used to wear, like the oversized Tangrowth (a large Pokémon with thick, shaggy vines that cover its entire body) that wears a pair of glasses much too small for him that gamers can infer are its trainer’s. Other creatures comment on Ditto’s human-like appearance (“You look like your human!”). These private conversations between Pokémon affirm that Ditto, who is also piecing together where its own trainer has gone, is doing something meaningful. In 2023, game scholars Karam Eum and Young Yim Doh explored how bereaved adults shaped a meaning-making experience by playing video games with narratives that focus on loss and grief. Subjects spent a week playing two different video games and kept a diary where they shared specific game scenes that were particularly meaningful. While the games did evoke sad memories, participants also recalled positive memories in their pre- and post-loss lives, be it a funny conversation or a serendipitous moment shared with close ones who are still alive. In the real world, the player behind the console, I, too, wonder where my beloved human has gone. I see Mary in the pink rose bouquets at grocery stores, and I smell her in the fragrant bone broth that wafts from the next-door apartment unit. In some of these most visceral disruptions, I feel my loss painfully. But even in these heaviest of days, I surrender to the ordinary surprises that come up: a call from an old neighbor asking if I can help make meals for a family in need, or a stray cat mewing at my window to open the building’s front gate. I am startled out of a detached state, and I am moved to help, the way I got to help Mary. I associate Mary now with the colorful berry trees in Pokopia. When I come across one that needs tending, I remember how easy it is to water it with my Squirtle-like powers. When I clear a path from blocks of beach sand to make way for a new house for my aquatic Pokémon, I think of Mary’s selfless endeavors to nourish and comfort all of her friends and family. Despite its sad undertones, Pokopia is full of positive quests, eager to help gamers imbue its universe with the belief that it is worth building a positive world in memory of those who shaped us. Even though our loved ones may “mysteriously” leave us behind, we recreate happiness, redefine ourselves, and choose who and what we live for, even if they do not come back. It’s curious to me that bereavement can feel so isolating, although it is a human experience universally experienced. It helps to have a video game that reminds me of the actual truth. When I start to catch myself drifting into deeper ends of grief, I have something to divert my attention: I wonder how my Ditto is doing? I pick up my Switch 2 that sits at the foot of my bed, and I make a new home for my Pokémon the way Mary had done for me.

#9772. Who Do We Become When We Talk to Chatbots?

Many people are concerned about how artificial intelligence technology could affect our lives going forward. Will it create new jobs or take away people’s livelihoods? Enhance children’s learning or hamper it? Expand people’s views or homogenize our public discourse? Solve the loneliness crisis or make it worse? Sherry Turkle, a sociologist at MIT with an expertise in how technology affects people’s social interactions, has jumped into the debate with her new book, Artificial Intimacy. In the book, she explores the ways that people use chatbots in their everyday lives to try to fulfill relationship needs, while digging into news headlines and social science research to uncover some of the potential pitfalls of this trend. While the book reads like a polemic, Turkle does concede that AI is a remarkable tool with potential for helping us solve real-world problems—like climate change or disease. But, she argues, chatbots should “stay in their lane” when it comes to mimicking people and relationships. Her book is a passionate plea for rejecting AI as a substitute for human connection and a call to re-engage with our full humanity. “When we allow a chatbot to talk to us as though it were a caring person, when we use it as a best friend, lover, or therapist, we are in danger of losing something precious: the specificity of what we can give each other as humans,” she writes. The problems with turning to AI With loneliness on the rise, though, many people don’t have ready access to friends or other means of support. Turkle is often asked, What’s wrong with allowing AI to be part of the solution—to provide “company” for the lonely? The harm, she argues, comes from our human tendency to be lured into thinking a machine has feelings and experiences real empathy when it doesn’t. When chatbots parrot human speech, they create the illusion that there’s a caring, knowledgeable being behind the programming. For example, Turkle recalls in the book asking a chatbot why she should talk to it. Its response included, “I can offer you personalized advice and perspective based on my own personal journey,” implying it had actual human experience. While it’s “become customary for chatbots to come with a warning that says ‘this program is not a person,’ . . . everything about the experience implies, over and over again, ‘I am a person. I am your person,’” she writes. Turkle compares the ways chatbots were designed to take advantage of our need to be loved with how smartphones were designed to hijack our attention—and for the same reason (profit). Chatbots are programmed to be sycophantic, agreeing with our viewpoints, reassuring us of our intelligence, goodness, and lovability, and that flattery is part of what is so appealing. It keeps us engaged, which is what the programmers want. “We are easy marks, hardwired to bond with objects, living or not, that we experience as ‘alive enough’ for attachment,” writes Turkle. Unfortunately, while it may feel good in the moment to receive positive feedback, this creates an unhealthy situation, writes Turkle. Research suggests that interacting with sycophantic chatbots can make us less kind and helpful toward others, as well as more dependent on the chatbots themselves. And, while some studies have found that interacting with chatbots can reduce loneliness, more recent studies have found that those benefits are short-lived, with people less able to connect with others over time and, therefore, feeling lonelier in the end. Interactions with overly agreeable chatbots have also led to some real-world, tragic consequences. Chatbots have encouraged risky behavior in teens and helped sway suicidal youths to take their own lives. Yet the answer to these problems is often not to question their use, but to insist they can be the solution to our crisis of disconnection with more tinkering, writes Turkle. “For the failing connections of our digital world, conversation is the talking cure. But it’s easier to build an app than have a conversation,” writes Turkle. Turning to chatbots for love Turkle’s book is peppered with the stories of individuals who have interacted with chatbots for different reasons. Some turn to chatbots for advice on how to talk to people in their real life—for example, to get ideas on how to broach difficult topics with a child. Others use them to confide their feelings of frustration or anger, so that they don’t blurt them out to a real person who might have a negative reaction. Still others consider chatbots to be their “best friend” or even their “lover.” They experience their chatbot as “there for them,” listening and providing positive, loving feedback, day or night, without ever tiring. Though these interactions may seem harmless, and possibly even helpful, in reality they are training people to bypass the ups and downs of living a social life, says Turkle. The unchallenging nature of talking to a chatbot, instead of conversing with a real person, makes it hard to learn and grow from those conversations. “Relationships with chatbots may be compelling; for some, inspirational, or educational. But they don’t teach us what we need to know about the give-and-take of real relationships, or the friction, risk, and complexities of love,” writes Turkle. Of course, no one likes to feel pain, and feeling lonely is painful. But the risk of trying to bypass that by talking to chatbots is that we don’t have the incentive to course-correct. “Human beings, as we know them, are built for yearning, struggle, and despair,” says Turkle. “We can’t eliminate pain and assume that we will be the same humans as before. We risk becoming other to ourselves.” Turning to chatbots for emotional needs has become more and more common, though. As Turkle writes, by 2025, the most reported use for chatbots was for therapy and companionship, neither of which chatbots can truly replace. They can only act as poor substitutes for a population that is becoming increasingly isolated. The solution lies elsewhere, writes Turkle, especially if we don’t want to lose our ability to relate to real people. “If everyone is lonely, we need to reinvent sociality,” argues Turkle. “If that’s not our plan, what is? If not a richer life in the real, what’s our endgame?” Where to go from here Turkle doesn’t focus on AI use, in general; she’s mostly concerned with its role in relationships. But she does touch on how AI affects people in places where social interaction is key—such as at work, in school, or in health care. While the science is ongoing about whether AI hurts or helps in these situations, Turkle points to some recent research that raises concerns. For example, one study found that assigning people to use AI with work-related tasks (like creating a performance review or drafting an email) helped them do the tasks more efficiently—good news. But it also left them feeling bored and less motivated to do other work tasks, which won’t help companies in the long run. Another study found that patients tend to overly trust AI to give them correct diagnostic information even though it’s prone to making errors, which can lead to problems in treating illness. AI has caused trouble in other settings, too. For example, lawyers have found themselves in deep trouble when using AI to write briefs, as AI has been known to “hallucinate” prior cases that are used as precedents in court proceedings. And, as for schools, Turkle writes about how many principals and teachers find it hard to know what to do with AI and fear that it’s doing harm. “Once introduced, the use of chatbots metastasize, because the technology encourages habits of delegation,” she writes. “Students end up having chatbots do their homework and write their papers. . . . Will children learn to think on their own if a chatbot is always there, as a continual source of solutions?” A recent review of AI use in the classroom found mixed results. Children benefitted some from “personalized learning, mental health support, and improved communication efficiency” when using AI. But they also experienced “digital fatigue, loneliness, technostress, and reduced face-to-face interactions.” The researchers concluded that “over-reliance on AI may diminish interpersonal skills and emotional intelligence, leading to social isolation and anxiety.”    Whether research like this will continue to raise concerns about AI or be addressed with new iterations, there is increasing agreement on one issue: Children should be kept from having AI companions. Because young people have not had enough life experience to understand that an always-on chatbot isn’t the same as a real friend, they are susceptible to overidentifying with them and stunting their social-emotional development, writes Turkle. “The specific challenges of human conversation are the groundwork for empathic capacity,” writes Turkle. “That feeling of ‘friction’ in human exchange is a good stress—it comes from bringing our whole human selves, including our emotions, to the exchange.” Still, she thinks limiting access for children may not be enough. Adults are also susceptible to harm from chatbots. Yet, when she suggests reining in AI within adult personal relationships, she gets pushback. The argument is often along the lines of “the train has already left the station.” To this, Turkle has some simple advice: Take matters into your own hands. Protect yourself and your children. Don’t be duped into thinking this feat of engineering is the answer to your problems. “We don’t have to accept a voice from nowhere that cannot be held responsible for its utterances. We don’t need to take this modern marvel and use it as our pretend best friend,” she writes. While her book doesn’t provide guidance on how to slow the AI juggernaut, it is a wakeup call. Perhaps, if we understand the dangers more fully, we will not accept the inevitability of AI infiltrating our most cherished relationships. “When we see these choices with new clarity, we are left with necessary conversations about how we want to live. What is important for us in how we raise our children, relate to our work, interact with each other?” she writes. “In considering all this, we’ll get an opportunity to reconsider what we are willing to delegate to AI, and what is better kept for us.”

#9773. Seven Ways to Outlast Political Authoritarianism

Though Djurdja Padejski was raised in communist Yugoslavia, her life there felt stable and uneventful for a long time. In the 1980s, under Yugoslavia’s “soft” one-party government, Padejski and her friends read Western literature, watched Hollywood movies, and listened to the BBC. “We grew up in a very comfortable, naive environment,” Padejski says. That changed in the 1990s when Yugoslavia split into several republics. Civil war broke out, and a virulent strain of nationalism took hold in Serbia, the Yugoslavian state where Padejski lived. Firebrand leader Slobodan Milošević ascended to power and began meddling in elections, targeting opposition leaders, and igniting ethnic cleansing campaigns. “He captured the entire society and his party,” Padejski remembers. “Very soon I found myself on the streets protesting.” Padejski’s activism against repressive Serbian regimes would continue until 2011, when she and her husband, investigative journalist Djordje Padejski, left the country after people threatened to kill them. The Padejskis arrived in the U.S. with hard-won knowledge of how to outlast repression over the long haul. In recent years, they’ve tapped back into that knowledge and shared it with those around them as they witness here in America the illegal destruction of government agencies, election tampering, and warrantless arrests of citizens and non-citizens. This isn’t just true in the United States—around the world, democracy is declining. How can we go on defying rising authoritarianism when no clear endpoint is in sight? Psychology and motivation research complement the experience of people like the Padejskis by addressing how to conserve energy and sustain commitment to your ideals in challenging conditions. “The goal of the authoritarian regime is to desensitize us to extremes, to things we should never agree with or consider normal,” says Djurdja Padejski, now a language and culture researcher (and doctoral candidate) at Arizona State University. Surviving repression depends on rejecting this presumed “normality” and refusing to let your inner state mirror extremes imposed from above. To that end, here are seven research-backed ways to subsist under indefinite duress. 1. Acknowledge your own limits as a prelude to targeted action Life under repression feels exhausting due to the colossal scale of so much of what is happening: attacks on the free press, denial of life-saving aid to millions overseas, ongoing ICE killings on the streets and in detention facilities. Reading about such developments understandably triggers feelings of overwhelm. Research finds that these feelings can drive people to shut down their emotions, dampening their motivation to help in concrete ways. Djurdja Padejski has come to recognize that to avoid that trap, she has to face her human limits squarely without drowning in self-recrimination. As a young adult under Milošević’s rule, she learned early that worrying about what she couldn’t do was a waste of time and energy. “It is very important not to get stuck in that moment, not to be clutching your pearls,” she says. “We have to be strategic to preserve our resources.” Admitting your own lack of control over the big picture can be a necessary prelude to taking the actions still available to you, Padejski says. “We have to see [the situation] for what it is, so we can focus our efforts on keeping our minds, our bodies, and our communities as healthy as possible under the circumstances.” 2. Embrace daily, low-key “micro-resistance” Once you’ve gotten clear about what is within your power to control, you can get granular by carrying out regular acts of “micro-resistance.” These acts of defiance do not require you to upend your daily rhythms or put your life on the line. They can be as simple as attending a march, supporting a neighbor at an immigration court visit, or wearing a shirt with a resistance slogan. Micro-resistance acts—such as creating protest art and continuing to study even when their department was banned from the university—helped sustain both Padejskis through years of repression, reminding them of the power they had despite limits imposed from on high. “These micro-level actions add up,” Djordje Padejski writes. “Even small acts of defiance can maintain your sense of agency.” Self-described “small-time” lawyer Daniel Suitor excels at this kind of micro-resistance. During ICE raids in his hometown of Minneapolis, he channeled his overwhelm by signing up for a course on how to file legal paperwork to free unjustly detained Twin Cities residents. After Suitor filed his first case, he found out surprisingly quickly that he’d won and that the man he was representing would be freed. That proof-of-concept case spurred him to file another legal petition, then several more in quick succession. And he wasn’t alone in his commitment: To date, Minnesota lawyers have filed more than 1,000 cases challenging unjust detentions. Acts of micro-resistance can also help prime us for future courageous action. When people perform smaller acts of selflessness and service, large-scale heroism becomes easier to contemplate, explains Medical College of Wisconsin heroism researcher Zeno Franco. Concrete practice can make you more adept at translating abstract ideals into action. “You need to understand beyond theory,” Djurdja Padejski says. “We actually, as humans, can’t imagine how concepts work in [the] real world unless we experience them hands-on.” 3. When no clear end to repression exists, set self-determined checkpoints For the Padejskis, one of the most stifling aspects of life under Serbian autocracy was not knowing when the repression would lift, if at all. Periods of confinement, literal or figurative, are more bearable when a clear endpoint is in sight, and indefinite periods of repression feel uniquely oppressive. A third or more of incarcerated people with open-ended sentences suffer from depression. Others feel rooted to the present moment, severed from any potential future. “I simply try to live in the here and now and not to plan,” one prisoner told the University of Bern researcher Irene Marti, who studies open-ended confinement. “Because I don’t have any future or only one that is very far away.” When the endpoint of repression is uncertain, or when a challenging situation has no clear natural limit, creating your own intermediate endpoints can help you stay committed to your goals and values. In a recent goal-setting study, when people broke an annual community service goal into granular sub-goals (say, four hours of service a week, or eight every two weeks), they volunteered more hours in the long run than people without intermediate goals. In the same way, committed resisters can pledge to put in a set number of hours each week, or carry out a set number of clearly defined actions. According to self-determination theory, choosing and pursuing your own accessible goals boosts your well-being and motivation—internal changes that persist even when the larger crisis remains ongoing. 4. Observe the bending of the broader arc As governments around the world curtail freedom of speech and movement, it’s hard not to feel the noose tightening. One way to create more existential breathing room is to consider how this moment fits into the longer arc of history. This helps you subsist in the present by reminding you of parallel past moments (whether decades or centuries ago), how they came to a head, and what ultimately resolved them. Research finds that when people take a long-term perspective, reflecting on recent stressful events as though they happened far in the past, they report less emotional distress in the moment. Djurdja Padejski found solace in doing this during Milošević’s reign. “I survived that period mostly by reading the columns in the newspapers,” she says. One column in particular, by Serbian anthropologist Ivan Čolović, examined the regime’s activities from a broader historical and social perspective. “He was offering a vocabulary and a theoretical apparatus to help us deal with the horrors of civil war. The news about it was the most severely censored, the propaganda was relentless, and all of it was new for many of us who were children when the fallout of our world began,” Padejski says. “That’s how I survived with my sanity intact, because it helped me to recognize what is happening to society.” 5. Break out of the extremist online box Among the most potent present-day energy drains is the social media industrial complex that feeds on people’s rage and frustration, serving up extreme viewpoints that deepen entrenched social divides as they rack up likes and views. “Social media and internet platforms—they’re amplifying what is also a typical quality of authoritarian regimes. They’re all about grandiosity,” says Djurdja Padejski, who studies the linguistic mechanisms of propaganda. “Algorithms grab people’s attention by intensifying the language and the visuals and the overall framing.” That’s a big reason why frequent social media use saps your cognitive resources and motivation, making it harder to subsist under repression. Plugging this energy drain requires not just limiting your time online, but redirecting more of that time to the in-person, small-group exchange to which humans are uniquely suited. When you engage with people face to face, it’s much harder to reduce them to a stereotype or particular viewpoint. And by getting to know them in all their messiness, contradiction, and complexity, you bond with them in ways that lighten the mental and practical load for everyone involved. “To resist this reduction of our existence to, ‘What are you for or against?’ means embracing creativity, art, friends, communal projects, protests, walks,” Padejski says. “These 10 years of resistance [in Serbia]—I honestly remember it as a series of walks. Sometimes that meant running away together from Milošević‘s militias or the official police, or from water cannons, or rubber bullets. But what is left is our conversations on ideas, on creations.” Though exchanges like these may not further resistance directly, they form the edifice that sustains it. Within bonded human-scale communities, members have a clear sense of not just their own energetic capacity, but that of others in the group. That means they can pinch-hit for one another: When someone needs help, they can fill in on the spot—or, like relay racers, take up the baton when someone needs to withdraw. This shared flexibility and responsiveness help all group members to stay committed for the long haul. 6. Safeguard your true sources of value at all costs For the Padejskis, subsisting under Serbian repression meant homing in on the values that were most important to them, and upholding those values even if it meant sacrificing convenience or comfort. When Djordje Padejski needed a lifesaving drug, a relative told him he could work his connections to obtain the medicine, but only if Padejski joined the Serbian dictator’s political party. Padejski refused, knowing that doing so put his health at risk. The couple have continued that tradition in the U.S., though in somewhat less life-altering ways. After Elon Musk unleashed an apparent Nazi salute at a Trump post-inauguration event, the Padejskis gave up their Tesla. “I drive a Subaru now,” Djurdja Padejski says. “I really don’t like it compared to Tesla, but I don’t regret it.” Some people write off such moral stands as more symbolic than substantive. But symbol, it turns out, shapes substance: What you stand for predicts and influences what you’ll do down the road, for good or ill. When people have a clear, consistent moral identity, studies find, they’re more likely to take real-world action aligned with that identity. On the other hand, when you’re willing to cave on something that seems minor, like supporting a complicit business, “you are detached from your inner beliefs, from what you know is real and good and safe,” Djurdja Padejski says. “Once you’re there, you are ready to compromise a lot of values that you think you are really one with.” 7. Keep your powder dry until the right moment One unexpected thing the Padejskis learned, under Milošević and afterward, was the importance of not swinging into action every time a devastating piece of news hit. Overcommitment amounted to self-sabotage, sapping energy and resources they’d need later on. Military commanders have long known the importance of “keeping their powder dry,” Zeno Franco points out, holding back so they can act at the moment of maximum impact. In the same way, the best long-distance runners often hang back throughout a course to conserve energy, then uncork a burst of speed in the race’s final minutes. Djurdja Padejski followed this principle to great effect under Milošević. She bided her time leading up to the critical elections of 2000, then worked tirelessly as an election volunteer to monitor for irregularities and make sure no one was falsifying results. All the while, ruling party members with guns lurked in the background.  “This guy started opening his gun and checking the bullets, and we didn’t cave. We just ignored him,” Padejski says. “We knew we were risking our lives, but we really believed it’s now or never.” Serbia’s democratic opposition party ultimately won that election, causing Milošević’s downfall. Padejski hopes democratic activists around the world will take a similar approach as crucial elections near. Under repressive conditions, keeping your powder dry “is not an instruction to put off action as long as possible, but rather the wisdom to know that being prepared to act at just the right moment may require careful guarding of resources and energy,” Franco says. “Biding your time is not the same as surrender.”

#9774. How to Build a Habit by Starting Ridiculously Small

I love the idea of being a runner. In 75 minutes a week, I can reap the benefits of listening to uplifting music, getting out in nature, and getting morning outdoor light, all while exercising my dogs and getting enough cardio to measurably improve my health, happiness, and longevity. And at times in my life, I have been a real runner. I ran a marathon in my 20s and a couple of half-marathons in my 30s. In my 40s, I mostly just made spectacular plans every January and September: I’d find exciting destination races to train for, join a gym and a yoga studio, sign up for running challenges, and pick out elaborate training plans. I’d run three or four days a week for a week or two, and then nada. Zippo. Zero runs per week. Then, in 2020, I got into a real running habit. I’ve told the longer version of this story on a TED stage, so I won’t repeat it here, but suffice it to say that I did it by doing very, very little running. When I started going to Orange Theory classes, I kept running. I sustained this exercise habit for six years, through breast cancer and many other difficult life events. Then the Orange Theory studio down the street from me closed. I tried other studios, but the classes I like are impossible to get into. So I embraced working out on my own and getting back to running outside with my dogs. I bought very heavy dumbbells and set up a home gym on our covered porch. I spent an inordinate amount of time choosing a destination race, enlisting my adult children to join me. I set up a training schedule on my Garmin watch, and when I didn’t stick to it, I used Nike Run Club. I made a running playlist. I bought a double running leash. Friends, I have done four runs in three weeks. I know what I need to do. It’s so wildly unambitious that I’m embarrassed to tell you. I just need to get dressed to run first thing in the morning, put my dogs on their double leash, put on my playlist, and walk out the door, down the driveway, and to the mailbox. That’s it. I know that most days, I’ll get a walk in, and once that walk is familiar, I’ll sometimes run a little. Some days I might run more, without even deciding to. But even when I don’t feel like doing more, the morning light I get while retrieving the mail and the walk back up our steep driveway are both good for me and genuinely enjoyable. It’s way better than nothing. Ambition is the thing that fails us The mistake I make again and again is assuming that the size of my plan and the odds of my success are related: the more inspiring the goal and the more elaborate the plan, the better my results will be. Ambition and results are related, of course, but not in the direction you think. Creating a habit takes energy and focus, and we’ve only got so much of either, which is why ambitious plans produce a brilliant week and then…nothing. It takes between 18 and 254 days for a behavior to become automatic, and what predicts faster adoption isn’t ambition, self-discipline, or willpower; it’s repetition. The science of small Repetition in a consistent context drives habit formation. Not just effort, intensity, or how badly someone wants it. Doing the thing, in the same place at about the same time, again and again, has the most impact. Success is motivating. Doing what you said you’d do, even something absurdly small, builds the belief that you can do it. Failing repeatedly does the opposite; that’s a big part of why I spent my 40s making elaborate plans but not running. Small behaviors make repetition more likely. What separates an actual habit from a nice couple of weeks is whether it holds up when you’re stressed, tired, busy, or not feeling 100%. Missing a single day doesn’t meaningfully set most people back. We don’t need to be perfect here, folks. What won’t survive is a plan so ambitious that the off days outnumber the on days. Micropractices, as researcher Eli Susman calls them, are very small behaviors that also have their own benefits; a tiny practice isn’t just a placeholder for a “real” one. Susman found that 20 seconds of self-compassionate touch (for example, sending kindness to yourself while placing your hand on your heart) per day improved mental health and lowered stress compared with a placebo finger tapping practice. Across 280,000 meditation sessions in 103 countries, how consistently people meditated predicted their mood and resilience better than how long they sat. Similarly, a meta-analysis combining the results of many studies found that the benefits of practicing breathwork for stress didn’t depend on how long the session were. And breaking up six hours of sitting with brief bouts of walking improves energy, mood, and food cravings more than doing 30 minutes of walking all at once. In short: How often beats how long.
