Other concerns that impede supportive school climates include health and safety crises such as the pandemic, and student mental health concerns that are manifesting in dramatically changing student behavior. And the list goes on. What are educators doing to address these increasingly challenging pressures? What practical strategies are they implementing to rebuild positive, relationship-centered classrooms? Amid these pressures, many educators are creating hopeful new ways of rebuilding school climate, as we learned through our research. This article highlights promising, relationship-centered practices that educators are using right now to foster belonging, productive classroom discussions, conflict resolution, and emotion regulation for both students and themselves. Connection before correction: Cultivating a sense of belonging Sarah, a fifth-grade teacher we encountered in our research, notices that Maeve often acts out on test days. As a math test is about to begin, Maeve becomes disruptive—bothering classmates, crumpling papers, and making accusatory comments. Sarah can either immediately correct the behavior or first connect with Maeve. She chooses connection. Sarah pulls Maeve aside and asks how she’s feeling about the test. Maeve admits she’s nervous and afraid of failing. Sarah listens, reassures her that mistakes are part of learning, and shares that she gets test jitters, too. Feeling seen and supported, Maeve calms down. Only then does Sarah briefly address the earlier behavior, recognizing it was rooted in Maeve’s anxiety. According to a survey our team administered to nearly 100 educators, teachers are utilizing practical ways to prioritize connections through strategies such as warm individual greetings when students enter the classroom, empathy and validating feelings, sharing personal stories about their lives, and showing sincere interest in students’ lives. By actively listening, using reassuring words and facial expressions, and recognizing student hobbies and activities, they establish common ground for building caring relationships. Saying something as simple as “I understand how you feel” can build a bridge. Research indicates that when students are in a state of “belonging uncertainty,” they may question the quality of their social connections, directly impacting their motivation. A compromised sense of belonging can cause students to interpret tasks as more difficult than they are as they align the challenge with a feeling that they simply don’t belong. Connecting before correcting can bolster students’ view of problems as more temporary and typical than permanent and unsolvable. Empowering student voice Soon after the murder of George Floyd in June 2020, numerous graduates from a suburban high school reached out to their hometown school district’s curriculum office. Across their college classrooms, cafeterias, and dorm rooms, discussions about race in modern America were intense. All of the alumni who reached out felt ill-prepared to engage in such discussions. The director of curriculum shared this feedback with the secondary social studies and English language arts (ELA) teachers. The teachers responded with fear and concern. They were worried that facilitating classroom discussions around controversial and emotionally charged topics, such as race, would lead to microaggressions, hurt feelings, and repercussions for teachers as a result. Classroom discussion driven by student voice, where students share their ideas and learn from one another, can sometimes be counterproductive. Research indicates that teachers require explicit professional learning to design and sustain structured student voice practices, including strategies for eliciting authentic input, facilitating deliberation, and engaging students as co-designers of the practices. To achieve this, the secondary social studies and ELA teachers received training in creating structured environments for discussion using evidence-based best practices:Co-create classroom norms: Let students set the norms and rules for class discussion. Scaffold respectful civil discourse: Structured techniques like Yes-No-Maybe and Respectful Debate teach students to articulate opinions, listen deeply, and reflect on others’ ideas. Practice audience-focused communication: Students tailor their messages for different audiences—classmates, teachers, administrators, or community members. This framework validates all students’ voices and choices and tends to increase their internal motivation, while maintaining a safe and structured environment. Structured, student-driven open forums—such as Socratic seminars, student-led meetings, and restorative circles—help formalize respectful exchanges and cultivate trust so students feel safe to take intellectual, social, and emotional risks, all of which are foundational conditions for productive discussion in and out of the classroom. Co-regulation as the foundation for student-centered discipline After a peer conflict in algebra class, a middle school student visited the counselor’s office, displaying signs of emotional distress—rapid breathing, tense shoulders, and defensive responses. Instead of asking questions about the situation or reinforcing expectations about appropriate behavior, the counselor sat next to the student, and in a calm tone of voice said, “It looks like you’re feeling frustrated, so let’s slow things down and take a few deep breaths together.” After the student’s breathing slowed and their tense shoulders softened, the counselor asked several open-ended questions to help the student reflect on and make sense of the experience and determine a clear plan of action to repair the peer conflict. Self-regulation involves a wide range of skills, including emotional awareness, self-soothing, and flexible thinking. Students learn how to self-regulate through co-regulation, the bidirectional process between a child and adult, in which a calm and caring adult provides the environment and scaffolds for the child to regulate their emotions and behavior during stressful situations. When a child experiences feelings of anxiety or overwhelm, their nervous system enters “fight or flight” mode, which may manifest as an emotional meltdown or withdrawal. Through the calm and steady presence of an attuned adult modeling regulation techniques, the balance of their nervous system can be restored. One of the counselors we surveyed draws upon the 3 Rs from psychiatrist Dr. Bruce Perry with her students:Regulate: Help the student to use exercises such as deep breathing or rhythmic movement to reach a calm state. Relate: Connect with the student through active listening and empathy to understand their perspective. Reason: Invite the student to engage in reflection and problem solving. Perry’s steps are grounded in neuroscience, acknowledging that the lower brain must be regulated first before students can access higher brain regions, such as the cortex, to carry out activities like reasoning. Over time, engaging in co-regulation with caring adults fosters whole-brain integration, reduces the long-term impacts of stress, and helps children to build resilience by internalizing emotional regulation skills through safe, supportive relationships. Restorative practices to build accountability After two students got into a heated argument during a group project in a seventh-grade social studies lesson, one student slammed her laptop and said, “That’s it! I give up! You do everything your way.” The other student rolled her eyes and continued typing. Rather than sending the students to the main office or school counselor, the teacher invited them both into the hallway for a restorative conversation. She asked them each to share their perspectives and then to consider who was affected by their actions. After each student had an opportunity to share, she guided them toward repair and resolution by asking them, “What can you do to improve the situation?” and “What do you need to do that?” Within five minutes, both students verbally agreed to apologize to their fellow group members, as well as to clearly define everyone’s roles within the group. The restorative conversation allowed the girls to resolve their conflict, strengthen their relationship, and shift the focus from blame to accountability. Restorative practices like this conversation consist of formal and informal processes that help to resolve conflict, foster relationships, and build a sense of community. Numerous studies find that exclusionary forms of discipline, such as suspensions, have detrimental effects on students, including heightened mental health challenges, increased misbehavior, and lower academic achievement. Restorative practices offer a relationship-centered alternative that empowers students who have committed wrongdoing to take full accountability by understanding how their behavior affected others, acknowledging the harm it caused, and taking meaningful action to repair the harm. Whether used formally through restorative circles and conferences or informally with restorative conversations and talking about feelings, when students are exposed to restorative practices, research finds they show benefits such as experiencing decreased bullying and violence, improved mental health, fewer suspensions and expulsions, improved behavior, and restored relationships. One school administrator we interviewed said, “I have found that schools who use restorative practices encourage stronger bonds and relationships between teacher and students and between students themselves. This sense of community is a vital facet of a positive school culture and climate.” Routines to promote educator wellness and connection Just before, during, and after the COVID-19 pandemic, the emotional labor demands on teachers increased to levels unseen in history. In an effort to mitigate the emotional distress of his teachers, one principal asked each staff member to give a positive shoutout to a colleague in the first few minutes of every staff meeting. This routine did lift the mood momentarily and promoted greater positivity, but there was still a great deal of emotional turmoil under the surface that was unaddressed. Just as students benefit from adult co-regulation that scaffolds their ability to regulate their emotions in stressful situations, so do teachers. An effective co-regulation strategy that works for principals aiming to co-regulate their staff’s emotional distress involves adult modeling and scaffolding of emotional regulation strategies. In the midst of COVID, the principal implemented a three-minute routine at the start of each staff meeting by modeling a strategy called structured SELf-Questioning:I do: The principal models via a think aloud, asking and answering out loud two questions (“What am I feeling?” and “What can I do to self-calm?”) We do: Staff ask themselves the same questions, write down their answers, share in pairs, and share out with the whole group if time permits. You do: Teachers replicate this in their classrooms with their students. As the emotional challenges facing schools intensify, the steady ability to navigate one’s own emotions while helping others do the same is the key to a principal’s success. Providing teachers with evidence-based adult SEL experiences—such as those embedded into staff meetings or professional development—can meaningfully improve educator well-being and promote greater engagement. Abundant research finds that SEL training and SEL teaching have major benefits for both teachers and students. Reclaiming relationships as the heart of schooling The pressures facing today’s schools are real, complex, and often overwhelming. It is becoming increasingly clear that meaningful change does not need to start with expansive policy revisions or punitive approaches; it begins with relationships. Across classrooms, counseling offices, and staff meetings, educators and administrators are choosing connection over correction, regulation over reaction, and restoration over exclusion. They are prioritizing belonging, elevating student voice within classroom environments, engaging in co-regulation, utilizing restorative practices, and tending to educator wellness. While these approaches are not quick fixes, they are grounded in research, brain science, and the lived experiences of educators who understand that behavior is a form of communication. When students and educators feel seen and heard, they take more academic risks; and when conflict is approached with repair instead of punishment, relationships thrive. Rebuilding school climate is not about returning to what used to be; it is about shaping how we will move forward, intentionally. And these educators and administrators remind us that the path forward is always better with connection.

#9800. What Happens When We Stop Trying to Win a Difficult Conversation?

It’s become a common story: Divided by politics, my father and I grew more and more distant, and after a while we could not talk about much without tension rising between us. He passed before that distance was ever repaired. I did not have the tools in time. I kept wondering why this happened so easily, to families and friendships and people who had once known how to be in the same room together. Then I went to graduate school at Colorado State University. I became a graduate teaching assistant and facilitator for the Center for Public Deliberation (CPD), which became the most fulfilling part of my time in graduate school. Part of what drew me to this work was my experience with my father, though our story is one many people will recognize. In recent years, political division has worked its way into kitchens and living rooms across the country, coming between people and making once-ordinary conversations hard to have. You may have felt it at your own table. When I found the CPD, I found the tools I had needed so desperately and had not known existed. These were not techniques for winning an argument or even holding your ground. They were ways of reaching the “why” underneath another person’s perspective, the experiences and stories and information that had built it. The goal, I discovered, should never have been agreement. It was understanding, the point where someone’s view makes sense to you as something a person could reasonably arrive at, even when reaching that point is hard. That was exactly what my father and I had never had a way to give each other. It showed me the path I wanted to take in life. Even beliefs that look irrational on the surface usually trace back to a real concern or value underneath. Understanding that root does not mean every belief holds up equally against facts. Some do not. What it means is that curiosity about the person’s underlying concern is what makes deep value-based conversation possible. You can respect where someone is coming from and still hold the line on what is true. The CPD is hired by cities and local organizations across Northern Colorado to bring community members together around shared challenges. Rather than holding traditional town halls, where people often talk past one another, the CPD designs events that foster collective wisdom: the recognition that the knowledge of a group is far more powerful than any single person’s alone. What I loved most was who filled the room. A retiree and a college student, a small business owner and a recent arrival, people from across the political spectrum and from neighborhoods that may rarely interact. The issues were as varied as the people: housing, how communities care for their aging members, student mental health, and, with the local newspaper, misinformation and political polarization. Whatever the topic, the aim was the same: to give a group what it needs to work through a shared problem, together. As a facilitator, my role was to help people, using techniques like uncovering the value tensions underneath their disagreements. This is more delicate than it sounds. When people argue, they tend to argue about conclusions, but conclusions are not always where the real disagreement lives. More often we hold many of the same values and simply weigh, rank, and apply them differently, shaped by what our own lives have taught us. Take accessory dwelling units (ADUs), small additional homes built on existing properties. One neighbor might prioritize privacy, worried that an ADU next door changes the quiet street or brings people they don’t know closer to their daily life. Another might prioritize freedom, believing homeowners should have the right to build on their own land as they see fit. Both values can be held by reasonable people. Neither is wrong. The disagreement isn’t really about ADUs. Instead, disagreement lives in which value, privacy or freedom, someone weighs more heavily when the two come into tension. Naming the value underneath a position does two things at once: It shows the group where the actual tension sits, and it makes the other person’s view legible as something a reasonable person could hold. Once you can see that, movement becomes possible. We were not necessarily searching for common ground that already existed. But, rather, we were building it together. At some point, tools stopped being something I reached for and became something I embodied in everyday life. I noticed it less as a single moment and more as a pattern showing up everywhere. I found myself curious in interactions that used to pass by unnoticed, wanting to know more about people for no reason other than wanting to know them. When someone said something I disagreed with, my first instinct stopped being to argue. It became asking what shaped that belief, what experience or value sits underneath it, digging past the kind of surface-level exchange most conversations settle for. Curiosity tends to be contagious. When I show someone true interest, they often show it back. What started as one person asking questions turns into two people learning from one another. That only works, though, if it stays voluntary. Some people are private, and that is their right. I am not asking anyone to disclose more than they want to. If someone wants to share, I listen. I ask. I learn. It stopped being a skill I used and became a way I move through the world. Long after the events ended, I would catch myself in a heated debate at a dinner or with friends, reaching for the why underneath what someone had said instead of what I deemed to be disagreeable or flawed. I stopped needing to win. I started assuming the person across from me knew something I did not, and that searching to discover their reasoning was the most respectful thing to do. The harder lesson was patience, and most of it was with myself. Some conversations made me frustrated. Some I simply got wrong. The discipline was never to pretend otherwise but to notice the mistake and repair it, and then to extend that same grace outward. Other people arrive carrying their own frustration and stumble in their own ways, and they need the room to learn that I needed, too. Sometimes the best thing I had to offer was curiosity. It was a single conversation across difference that left someone feeling heard, which can feel rarer than it should. That curiosity follows me well beyond any event, into encounters that have nothing to do with policy. In an old mining community in Wyoming that had largely emptied out, I stopped to talk with a man who made pottery. The town was the kind of place where the buildings tell the story before any person has to, where you can read the initial boom and the migration in the empty storefronts and homes. We sat together while he told me what it had been like to watch his town change, the prosperity that came and went, the quiet the exodus left behind. He spoke about staying when others left, about his craft, about what it means to keep making something with your hands in a place that feels as though some had forgotten. I was not there as a researcher and I had no agenda. I simply wanted to know, and so I asked, and he told me. I think about that conversation more than he would ever guess, and it gave me something no book could impart. This is what curiosity does. It lets you hear about other people’s lifetimes rather than just their opinions, and it asks something of you in return: the willingness to admit that your own understanding is incomplete, and that other people’s stories and experiences are part of what fills in the gaps. This requires us to choose curiosity over certainty, again and again, even when certainty would be easier. I do similar work now at Unify America, helping design and support civic assemblies that bring everyday people together to address shared problems. The timeline is slightly longer than those evening events in graduate school, but the ethic is the same. What moves me most still happens in the small moments of connection: people who would never have crossed paths in ordinary life, of different ages and neighborhoods and political realities, sitting down together over weeks and slowly getting to know one another. They share meals. They learn the names of each other’s kids. They discover, often to their own surprise, that a person they assumed was their opposite carries many of the same worries and hopes they do. I have been in the room while it happens, and you can feel the shift when it comes. The distance that seemed permanent reveals itself as circumstantial. I don’t say this to suggest that dinner-table tension is the whole story. Many people are living through a period of real precarity right now, for reasons that differ sharply depending on who you ask and what news you consume. Some people are afraid for their safety. Some are afraid for their livelihoods. Some are afraid of being unheard or unseen in a country that no longer feels familiar to them. Whatever the reason, whether it belongs to you, your neighbor, or a stranger you pass on the street, it comes from the same place. We are all moved by our hopes, our dreams, our values, our experiences, our fears. That’s part of what makes this work harder than it looks from the outside. You cannot ask someone to be curious about a neighbor’s perspective while pretending the stakes are small. The fear is real, even when two people in the same room are concerned about two completely different things. Deliberation does not erase that concern or resolve it. But it does something else that matters as much. It reminds people that they are not just living alongside one another, they are co-creating the communities they share. That means everyone has a seat at the table, not to win an argument, but to build something together that helps all of us navigate life the best way we know how and become a little more of the people we hope to become. Belonging grows out of that kind of collaboration. It is hard to build when we default to blaming our neighbors for our differences, and it gets easier when we choose curiosity instead, again and again, until it becomes a way of being with each other rather than a technique we reach for. The same attitudes carry weight beyond any single conversation. When people practice curiosity and patience with each other, they build the capacity to do something bigger together: to take part in shaping the decisions that govern their shared life. Civic processes work best when the people inside them can actually hear one another, weigh trade-offs, and hold a disagreement without needing to end it in conquest. That capacity does not appear on its own. It is built the same way any skill is built, through patience, in rooms like the ones I have described, and in the ordinary conversations that happen everywhere else. The more of us who carry these attitudes in public life, the more capable we become, together, of building a collective vision of what it means to participate in a shared civic life. This is why I do this work. Not because I am trying to solve polarization as an abstract problem, but because I know what it feels like when understanding breaks down between people you care about, and what it feels like when it can be repaired. Every assembly, every conversation, every moment of curiosity is a chance to help others experience what I am still learning myself: that we do not have to choose between our perspectives and our relationships. We can seek to understand one another not out of self-interest, but because human experience is rich beyond measure, and we are all a little incomplete without each other’s stories.

#9801. What Happens When Sixth Graders and Elders Learn Together

In Saskatoon, Canada, a group of sixth graders spend their school year outside their normal classroom and at the Sherbrooke Community Centre, engaged in conversation and activities with the long-term care residents who live there. One day, they might be found journaling, another learning new technologies, or writing limericks with the “elders,” many of whom have dementia or other conditions. Or they might be gardening, helping out at the center’s gift shop, or working on an art project.  “From my work with elders, I learned stories, games, and how to become a better person,” said a student named Mya. It even relieved despair for one resident, Jodi, who said, “I went from a daily dread that I was still alive and breathing . . . to anticipat[ing] an awesome day that was full of life and joy.” Many extracurricular activities, sports, meetups, and other community programs bring people of similar ages together: children with children, teens with teens, adults with adults, seniors with seniors. But new research on an intergenerational program known as iGen suggests that these initiatives may be especially beneficial to our health and well-being. Finding meaning in connection For the study, the 25 participating students—as well as three elders and eight long-term care staff members—completed a variety of surveys. At the end of the day, they rated how meaningful their interactions with different groups of people were, how much fun and positive and negative emotions they had, and the quality of their physical health that day. They also reported how many kind and helpful actions they performed, and how strong their social connections and sense of belonging were. Everyone completed surveys for 10 straight school days in late September and early October, and then again in June. The results suggested that people were experiencing meaningful interactions between generations, and highly meaningful interactions with their peers of similar ages. While people didn’t become happier, healthier, or more kind overall over the course of the program, the researchers did observe changes on a daily basis. On days when people had more meaningful interactions, they tended to feel better, more connected, healthier, and kinder. This was true for both interactions with people the same age (like students with students, or elders with elders) and intergenerational interactions—although the benefits seemed more pronounced between generations than with peers. This link appeared even stronger toward the end of the program than near the beginning. In June, having meaningful interactions through iGen seemed to have an even bigger impact on people’s well-being than it did in the fall. The researchers, led by Jason D. E. Proulx, suggest this might be because students had deepened their relationships with elders by that point, or they were savoring their last few weeks together. The length and depth of this program—spending whole days together over 10 months—may have contributed to how powerful it was, the researchers suggest. Although this research only reflects one program, with a majority of white participants, it offers promising evidence that programs like this can help young and old alike, and comprise a valuable part of students’ education. Other research finds that interacting across generations can improve mental health and cognitive function in older people, and a sense of purpose for all of us. “Programs like iGen may be one effective and scalable intervention to foster more inclusive, meaningfully connected, and happier communities,” write Proulx and his colleagues. Mya’s mother Tanya said that she “built long-lasting friendships with the residents . . . but also learned the value and appreciation of life.” “It took those sixth-grade kids to bring life back into focus for me,” said Jodi, who had thought her life was over and sometimes wanted it to be. “iGen saved me.”

#9802. How to Prepare Students for Life’s Pivotal Moments

Developmentally, middle school students are beginning to ask questions about who they are and who they want to become. At the same time, they are increasingly having to navigate real and often complex situations—moments when they may feel pressure to fit in rather than to stand up or speak out for what they know is right. We are beginning to expect a lot from these pre-teens, whether it’s keeping a promise, taking responsibility, or showing courage. But too often, we have not given them the language, structure, or time to think through how to make the right decision in those moments that matter. Scientific research can help—believe it or not! There are a range of compelling studies that offer middle school educators a roadmap on what skills and mindsets students need to make the right decisions.   Self-determination theory emphasizes the importance of intrinsic motivation—and there are compelling data that middle school students are more likely to act consistently when their goals are personally meaningful, not externally imposed. In addition, David Yeager’s work on adolescent motivation highlights that young people are deeply driven by a desire for status and respect from their peers and mentors. When students feel respected and see themselves as capable of contributing to others, their motivation and behavior improve. More recently, research by Benjamin Rogers and his colleagues suggests that when individuals of all ages see their lives as part of a broader “hero’s journey,” they are more likely to act with purpose and be a force for good. Overall, researchers point to a clear and compelling insight: Students need opportunities to define who they are, who they want to become, and how their everyday choices—guided by their values—align with that identity. This insight directly informed a curriculum we designed for middle school students titled Moments That Matter.   The Moments That Matter curriculum is a program of the Griffin Institute at the National Medal of Honor Museum, based in Arlington, Texas. For middle school students, a “moment that matters” could be when they’re being pressured to lie, steal, or cheat or when they want to hang out with their friends, but their mom needs them to stay at home with a younger sibling. Tapping into their own personal interests and experiences, our mission as educators was to take middle school students on a journey of self-discovery, empowering them to explore two essential questions: Who am I? Who am I becoming for others? While students learn about the history of the Medal of Honor—the highest U.S. military decoration—the curriculum is primarily focused on four core values that have enabled ordinary Americans to do extraordinary things: commitment, integrity, sacrifice, and courage. Each student begins their journey by identifying their own superpowers, the unique strengths, talents, and skills they will need to become their best selves for others. Students are also encouraged to identify the superpowers of their classmates. At the core of these activities is an opportunity for students to discover how their unique strengths can connect to and support the values and goals that mean the most to them. One of the cornerstone moments of the curriculum is when students are encouraged to reflect and write down a set of goals that focus on who they want to become for others. Some students write they want to be the best friend they can be. Others say they want their parents to see them as trustworthy and responsible. After students have written about a specific goal that will enable them to be their best self for others, teachers guide them through a series of lessons on each of the four Medal of Honor values. The lessons do not focus on hypothetical scenarios but on how each student’s everyday choices and decisions directly connect and shape their personal goals and identity. Commitment: Turning goals into action Students are encouraged to reflect on commitments they have already made, whether it’s helping their parents with the dishes or wanting to be the best teammate. Students also collaboratively explore the reasons why it’s sometimes hard to keep their commitments (e.g., peer pressure, competing priorities, wanting to fit in). In addition, students learn how to develop positive habits and routines that will help them to consistently “live” their commitments. Each student then creates their own “commitment action plan.” The plan includes:a commitment they will implement within the next seven days; the habits and routines they will create; the challenges they may face; and a person they will ask to hold them accountable to their commitment (a friend, parent, teacher). Integrity: Aligning our values, words, and actions Students next learn why integrity is important in those moments that matter—and how integrity occurs when we align our beliefs, words, and actions. Students read and discuss different scenarios where someone has shown integrity (and the value or belief at the heart of that person’s integrity, such as compassion, friendship, or sportsmanship). Students then create their own “Integrity Action Plan.” They start by writing down their North Star values, the values and beliefs most important to them (e.g., honesty, fairness, kindness). Every student is also encouraged to come up with their own personal motto that they will repeat to themselves whenever they need to be reminded why it’s important to follow their North Star values. Sacrifice: Acting for others, not just ourselves Students next explore how sacrifice is almost always linked to a conviction or strong belief about who they want to be. Students also discover how empathy and putting yourself in someone else’s shoes can help them decide when to make a sacrifice. They write in their journal about a fictional character from a movie, show, or book who made a sacrifice and how empathy might have motivated the character to make that sacrifice. Each student then creates their own “sacrifice preparedness plan.” They first write down a particular conviction or belief (e.g., “I want to be caring”). Next, they reflect on those “moments” when they may need to make a sacrifice to stay aligned with their convictions and beliefs. A week later, students come together to reflect on two questions: How does making sacrifices impact people in your life? and How could you start making sacrifices for the things or people you care about? Courage: Acting despite fear, danger, or hardship At this moment in the Moments That Matter curriculum, students are eager to explore the three essential “tools” that produce courage in those moments that matter: commitment, competence, and confidence. They learn that:commitments push us to act with courage; competence is having the knowledge and skills to do something scary or difficult; and confidence is that internal feeling of “I can do this!” —an indispensable tool that fuels our courageous behaviors. Students also learn about the power of positive self-talk. Finally, students are invited to write their own “courage action plan.” At the heart of the plan is identifying the different areas of their lives where they may need to act with courage. Next, they create their own “if/then” scripts: “If” I am in this situation, “then” I will respond with courage because I have made a commitment, practiced the skills and knowledge to do the right thing, and can leverage my confidence and positive self-talk to overcome my fears or doubts. What have we learned from tracking the classroom impact of Moments That Matter? Teachers who piloted the curriculum have told us that what they most appreciate about it is how it encourages student voice (e.g., how students were learning from each other as they shared with their peers why they sometimes did not show integrity or courage). The post-assessment data collected from our pilot implementation is also promising. Students report increased levels of self-awareness, a greater willingness to take responsibility for their actions, and a stronger tendency to think about their values when making everyday decisions. Perhaps most powerfully, we appreciated hearing from teachers that the Moments That Matter curriculum gave them the opportunity to serve as mentors. As one teacher shared with us: “The curriculum reminded me why I became a teacher. It gave my students space to talk about character in a way that felt authentic and meaningful for students—and me.”
